## Page 1


Affordable low-carbon electricity pathways for India under uncertainty
Guillermo Terr´en-Serrano1,2,*, Ranjit Deshmukh1,2,3,*,†, Srihari Dukkipati4, Measrainsey Meng2,
Shradhey Prasad2,3,5, Ana Mileva6, and Ashwin Gambhir4
1Environmental Studies Program, University of California Santa Barbara, Santa Barbara, CA, USA.
2Environmental Markets Lab (emLab), University of California Santa Barbara, Santa Barbara, CA, USA.
3Bren School of Environment Science & Management, University of California Santa Barbara, Santa Barbara, CA, USA.
4Prayas (Energy Group), Pune, MH, India.
5Marine Science Institute, University of California Santa Barbara, Santa Barbara, CA, USA
6Sylvan Energy Analytics, Portland, OR, USA.
*These authors contributed equally.
†Corresponding author: rdeshmukh@ucsb.edu
July 14, 2026
Abstract
To meet its carbon neutrality goal by 2070, India must accelerate the decarbonization of its electricity system.
However, uncertainty in technology costs and electricity demand, together with the need to balance high shares of
solar and wind generation, makes planning India’s electricity transition challenging. Here, we develop and examine
cost-optimal pathways for generation, storage, and transmission expansion in India under alternative technology
costs, electricity demand projections, and clean energy and carbon-emission targets. Across all scenarios, real average
system costs remain lower in all future years (2030-2050) than in 2020, even when carbon emissions decline linearly to
90% below current levels by 2050. Solar PV comprises over half to three-quarters of total installed capacity in most
scenarios. Large-scale deployment of short-duration battery storage provides both energy balancing and reliability
during net-peak demand hours. Expanding green hydrogen, pumped hydro storage, and nuclear capacity reduces
costs by less than 2%, whereas demand response programs reduce costs by up to 10%. Falling renewable energy and
battery storage costs along with demand response programs allow India to meet both electricity affordability and
climate goals.
Keywords — Battery Storage; Electricity System; Hydrogen Storage; India; Renewable Energy; Solar Energy; Wind
Energy.
1
Introduction
India, the world’s most populous and one of the fastest growing major economies, is the third largest greenhouse gas
(GHG) emitter, emitting approximately 3,000 million metric tons of carbon dioxide equivalent (Mt CO2e) in 2023
or 7.6% of annual global greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions [1]. With India’s average per capita energy consumption
below world average, its total emissions could substantially grow in the future depending on how the country provides
basic energy services, powers its growing economy, and adapts to climate change through space cooling and other
energy services. Recognizing this challenge, India made a pledge at the United Nations Climate Change Conference
in 2021 (COP26) to achieve carbon neutrality by 2070 [2]. Decarbonizing the electricity sector is a major strategy for
meeting this pledge because of the declining costs of wind, solar, and battery storage technologies, and the potential for
electrifying the transportation, industry, and other sectors [3]. However, the uncertainties in technology cost trajectories
and electricity demand forecasts, in addition to the weather-dependency of wind and solar resources, introduce new
challenges to planning and operating the electricity system. At the same time, climate targets need to be balanced
against energy affordability and access, especially in India with its relatively low average gross domestic product (GDP)
and GHG emissions per capita that are one-twentieth and one-sixth of the United States, respectively [1]. In this study,
we examine how technology costs and electricity demand trajectories affect electricity costs, GHG emissions, and new
infrastructure investment under alternative climate policy scenarios from 2030 to 2050.
Several studies have focused on India’s clean energy transition, especially in the power sector. Here, we examine
only those studies that explore India’s energy transition from 2040 and beyond. [4] implemented a carbon tax to model
a future (2050) 100% renewable energy system for India, projecting a cost decline of more than one-third compared to
the existing system. [5] developed bottom-up demand projections to examine the effect of low storage costs and high
1

## Page 2


air-conditioner efficiency on variable renewable energy (VRE) and storage deployment, and carbon emissions up to 2050.
Policy-driven scenarios have been explored as well, including India’s 500 GW target for non-fossil energy capacity and
its COP26 pledge for net-zero emissions by 2070 [6], and varying shares of renewable energy across multiple years [7, 8].
[5, 9, 10] examined the effects of technology cost projections, which are some of the biggest drivers of decarbonization.
Lastly, some studies have examined the role of specific clean energy technologies in addition to solar and wind, such as
green hydrogen [11] and nuclear energy [12].
Beyond differences in scenario assumptions, studies have produced divergent results for new infrastructure invest-
ments, system costs, and emissions, largely due to differences along two key dimensions: (1) input data assumptions
including technology costs and availability, demand projections, and clean energy and climate targets and (2) model
structure and constraints including temporal and spatial resolution and technical constraints. We provide a comparison
of these studies along these two dimensions in the Supplementary Tables 1 and 2 and expand upon the second dimension
below.
Because of computational tractability and data limitations, most studies use coarser single node [12, 13] or five zone
models [4, 5, 7, 8, 10, 11], which are inadequate for capturing the constraints on inter-state transmission that are crucial
for identifying and representing the spatial distribution of generation resources and electricity demand [14]. Only a few
studies use models that have state-level representation [4, 9, 15], a spatial resolution adopted in this study. To ensure
the reliability of the proposed electricity system by verifying that supply meets demand across all investment periods
[16], some studies adopt a high temporal or hourly resolution framework and simulate the electricity dispatch on 365
days with hourly resolution [4], but considerably reduce the spatial distribution [8, 11]. Other studies consider limited
representative days to improve model tractability while simulating multiple investment periods [7, 9], an approach we
adopt in this study. To ensure system reliability under varying weather conditions, we improve upon prior studies by
introducing a planning reserve margin (PRM) constraint, met by limited available capacities during peak demand hours
for each generator and storage asset, estimated from 18 years of weather data. Lastly, very few studies apply and share
open-source models [5, 8–10], which is important for accessibility, transparency, and reproducibility.
In this study, we develop an open-source electricity system planning and operations model—GridPath-India—using
the open-source GridPath power systems modeling platform [17]. The GridPath-India model has a spatial resolution
of 35 load zones associated with an hourly demand profile, one for each state and union territory, and one for the
neighboring country of Bhutan, a major hydropower exporter to India (Figure 1A). Transmission between zones is
limited by inter-state electricity transfer capacities, but transmission and distribution constraints within states are
ignored for computational tractability (Figure 1A). Existing, planned, and candidate generation and storage projects
for various technologies, including coal, natural gas, hydro (storage and run-of-river), nuclear, solar PV (both residential
and utility-scale), onshore and offshore wind, and battery, pumped hydro, and hydrogen storage, are represented in each
zone based on resource availability and plans. Over 1,300 candidate wind and solar sites are identified with a spatially
explicit, multi-criteria site suitability analysis (Figure 1B,C) [18]. The model imposes economic constraints, including
capital, fuel, and operations and maintenance costs, technical constraints including minimum and maximum capacity
factors, maximum capacity ratings, hydropower energy limits, storage round-trip efficiencies, and policy constraints
including renewable energy capacity, generation, and carbon emission targets. Using two representative days per month
to capture the peak and median daily demand with an hourly temporal resolution, we first co-optimize generation,
storage, and transmission investments and operations, including fuel and operations and maintenance costs across four
periods that each represent 10 years. The 2020 period (2015-2024) represents the existing system, whereas 2030 (2025-
2034), 2040 (2035-2044), and 2050 (2045-2054) periods represent near-term, medium-term, and long-term futures that
allow new investments. To ensure system reliability, we include a planning reserve margin that is met by effective load
carrying capacities (ELCCs) or limited available capacity during peak demand hours for each generation and storage
asset estimated using 18 years of weather data (Supplementary Figure 1).
To evaluate whether existing and new
infrastructure meets future electricity demand in all hours of the year, we simulate the hourly operations of the system
for all future investment periods using a production cost model.
We design three sets of scenarios that vary along three dimensions—(1) technology cost projections, (2) electricity
demand projections, and (3) clean energy and climate policy targets (Table 1).
For technology costs, we develop
low, mid, and high projections across 2020-2050 for all candidate clean energy technologies, including residential and
utility-scale solar PV, onshore and offshore wind, battery storage, and hydrogen, using India-specific and global data,
and combine them with two coal power plant capital cost levels (Figure 1F). For details about cost assumptions, see
Supplementary Note 4. To capture the uncertainty in electricity demand projections, we develop two sets of projections,
each with three different annual demand forecasts representing different levels of economic growth (low, mid, and high
with average hourly electricity demand for mid-growth scenarios shown in Figure 1E) [20]. One set includes bottom-up
hourly demand projections for the residential, commercial, transportation, industry, and agriculture sectors using the
PIER v2.0 model [19, 20]. Importantly, these demand projections reflect a shift in agricultural demand from evening
and nighttime hours to solar hours, representing India’s agricultural demand response program [21]. In addition, climate
change impact, specifically through increased cooling demand, is considered in residential buildings (see Supplementary

compared to carbon emissions in 2020. We report results for system costs, carbon emissions, new generation, storage, and
transmission capacity investments, and inter-state electricity trade. We provide a detailed description of the scenarios
in Supplementary Table 3.

Results
2.1
Costs, emissions, and clean energy shares
We evaluate our scenarios based on system costs and GHG emissions because of their relevance to consumer costs
and climate change mitigation. Average system costs normalize total system costs as electricity demand grows over
subsequent investment periods. Costs shown in this study include only generation, storage, and interstate transmission
investment and operations costs, and do not include intrastate transmission and distribution costs, which are assumed
to be the same across scenarios. Costs are expressed in real 2020 U.S. Dollar (USD) or Indian Rupee (INR) i.e., without
accounting for inflation between periods. Total GHG emissions allow comparison with 2020 emissions whereas the GHG
emissions intensity provides a measure of the climate impact per unit of energy generation. Lastly, clean energy share
estimates the share of non-fossil fuel sources, including solar, wind, biomass, hydropower, and nuclear, in total electricity
generation.
We first examine the effects of clean energy and carbon targets on India’s future electricity system (Figure 2A-E). In
the near-term (2030), meeting India’s official target of 500 GW of clean energy capacity results in a clean energy share
of 45% in line with India’s renewable purchase obligation (RPO) trajectory (43% by 2029-2030), but a 13% increase
in carbon emissions above 2020 levels (see all clean energy and carbon targets scenarios except the No Clean Target
or Carbon Target scenario in Figure 2A-E). However, a cost-optimal investment without a clean energy capacity target
shows greater deployment of solar and wind, with total clean energy capacity (790 GW, including 710 GW of solar and
wind and 80 GW of nuclear, hydro, and biomass) exceeding the official target by 57%. This No Clean Target or Carbon
Target scenario has a higher clean energy share of 69% (Figure 2E) and lower carbon emissions of 40% below 2020
levels (Figure 2C,D), while have a 6.8% lower cost (Figure 2A,B) in 2030 compared to the official target (Figure 2E).
However, because of on-the-ground constraints on land availability, relative slower pace of equipment manufacturing,
and transmission interconnection bottlenecks, which could all restrict the rate of clean energy deployment, the near-
term cost optimal result of the No Clean Target or Carbon Target scenario in this study may be difficult to achieve in
practice. Thus, across all other scenarios, we fix the clean energy capacity target at a maximum deployment of 500 GW
in 2030. Importantly, in spite of fixing the clean energy capacity target under these scenarios, average system costs in
2030 decline to USD 53.1 per MWh (INR 4.1 per kWh) compared to USD 56.9 per MWh (INR 3.8 per kWh) in 2020.
In the medium-term (2040), under the No Carbon Target scenario where no carbon cap is applied in 2040, total GHG
emissions fall by only 20% relative to 2020 because of tradeoffs between clean technology cost declines and an increase in
electricity demand. When a carbon target of 39% below 2020 levels is imposed, consistent with a linear carbon emission
reduction trajectory from 2030 emissions to the 90% target by 2050, the average system costs are remarkably similar
(less than 1% higher) to the No Carbon Target scenario (Figure 2B). Meeting this carbon emission reduction target
and assuming mid-trajectories for technology costs and electricity demand, average system costs and GHG emissions
intensity decline from 2030 levels to USD 45 per MWh (INR 3.3 per kWh) and 0.16 tCO2 per MWh, respectively, 21%
and 78% lower than in 2020. Clean energy capacity rises to 1,640 GW meeting 77% of electricity demand in 2040.
In the long-term (2050), cost and emissions differ substantially across scenarios because of varying carbon targets
applied in 2050. With No Carbon Target in 2050, GHG emissions remain relatively unchanged compared to 2040 and
decline by only 16% compared to 2020 (Figure 2C). Applying a carbon target in 2050, however, imposes additional costs
on the average system costs in 2050 for the 80%, 90%, and 100% Carbon Target scenarios increasing costs by 7.2%,
11%, and 22% compared to the No Carbon Target scenario (Figure 2B). Yet, across all climate target scenarios, the
2050 average system costs are lower than in 2020. For example, the 90% Carbon Target in 2050 results in costs of USD
47 per MWh (INR 3.4 per kWh), which is 17% lower than in 2020.

system. Because of the lower unit costs in 2050 compared to 2020 across all technology cost projections and demand
scenarios and its alignment with India’s economy-wide carbon neutrality goal by 2070, we set the 90% Carbon Target by
2050 as the reference scenario and the default climate policy for the technology costs and electricity demand scenarios,
thus fixing the upper limit for GHG emissions in 2040 and 2050.
VRE and storage technology costs impact system costs across all investment periods (Figure 2F,G). In contrast, coal
capital and variable costs have little effect on system costs because of coal’s diminishing share of capacity and energy
generation across all investment periods. Total system costs vary substantially across low to high VRE and storage
cost trajectories, increasing by 1.5-1.7 times (USD 130-140 billion or INR 9-10 lakh crore) in 2030, 1.9-2.5 times (USD
170-210 billion or INR 12-15 lakh crore) in 2040, and 2.4-3.6 times (USD 210-320 billion or INR 15-23 lakh crore) in
2050 compared to 2020. At the same time, average system costs are lower in all future investment periods relative to
2020 across all technology cost projections including the VRE & ESS (high) cost scenarios.
Demand growth projections have a limited effect on system costs (Figure 2K-L). Within each set of demand
projections—bottom-up and linearly-scaled demand patterns—total costs vary with annual demand growth projections
but average system costs are similar. However, demand projections based on bottom-up sectoral modeling result in lower
system costs (about 5.1-5.3% in 2040 and 8.1-9.8% in 2050) compared to the linearly-scaled demand projections that
assume no change to 2020 hourly demand patterns (Figure 2K,L). This decrease in costs is mainly driven by the shift
in agricultural demand to solar hours, a demand response program that enables a lower total generation capacity to
meet total electricity demand (see Supplementary Figure 6 and Table 10). Results for climate policy scenarios with
linearly-scaled demand profiles and alternative technology scenarios with bottom-up demand profiles are provided in
the Supplementary Figures 5–4 and Tables 8–7.

2.2
New capacity investments and energy generation
Fossil fuels, including coal, natural gas, and oil, dominate India’s existing electricity system, generating 75% of total
electricity demand in 2020 [22] (Figure 1A,D). That year, coal alone accounted for 235 GW (or 69%) of total installed
capacity and 990 TWh (or 71%) of total energy generation. An additional 12.6 GW of coal capacity was built from
2020 until 2025 and 24.2 GW is being planned and expected to be constructed by 2030 [23], all of which we include in
the 2030 coal capacity in our model. The model then builds additional coal capacity endogenously based on least-cost
principles. Power plants are assumed to retire at 45 years, which results in coal capacity retirements of 33.4 GW by 2040
and 50 GW by 2050. We first focus on the new capacity investments and energy generation across the technology costs
scenarios, which result in the most divergence in system costs (Figure 3). This set of scenarios assumes a 90% carbon
target in 2050 and electricity demand with the bottom-up profile and mid-growth projection. Results for the other
climate targets and demand projection scenarios are shown in Supplementary Figures 7 and 6 and Tables 11 and 10,
respectively.

Figure 3:
Technology capacity investments and energy generation and losses for technology cost projection
scenarios with a 90% carbon emissions reduction target by 2050. The new and existing generating capacity (MW) per
technology (A), new and existing energy capacity (MWh) for storage technologies (B), and electricity generation (positive), and
transmission and storage losses (negative) by technology (C) is shown for each period from 2020 to 2050. The hatch pattern
differentiates existing from new capacity (A, B). See Supplementary Table 9 for the underlying data.
Across the technology cost scenarios, which assume a 90% carbon target in 2050 and bottom-up mid-growth demand
projections that are more solar-aligned, limited fossil fuel generation capacity is built. Specifically, only 36.5 GW of
new coal capacity and 4 GW of new gas capacity is built by 2050 (Figure 3A). In fact, all the new coal capacity is
assumed in the pipeline (either planned or under construction) and no additional coal capacity is cost-optimally built by
the model in any period. Capital costs of new coal power plants has no effect on optimal new coal deployment. While
coal capacity is increasingly used to meet electricity demand during non-solar and non-windy hours, total coal energy
generation falls as the share of low variable cost renewable energy generation increases over time. As a result, capacity
factors of coal increase to 53% in 2030 before falling to 29%, and 4.7% in 2040 and 2050, respectively (Supplementary
Figure 10 and Table 9). Commensurately, the share of coal generation decreases to 50%, 17%, and 1.8% from 2030 to
2050 even as electricity demand quadruples by 2050.
If hourly demand profiles remain unchanged, i.e., linearly-scaled, which results in high demand peaks in the evenings,
then only a relatively small (up to 3 GW) additional new coal capacity is cost-optimally deployed whereas a substantially
large new gas capacity (59-74 GW or 3 times gas capacity in 2020) is installed (Supplementary Figure 6 and Table 10).
Natural gas power plants with their lower capital cost relative to coal power plants do not generate much energy (less
than 10% capacity factor) and are mainly used for balancing and reliability. Because India has not witnessed much
expansion of new gas capacity in the past few decades, we also examined a case where no new gas capacity is allowed.
In that scenario (No New Gas & ALT), an additional 47 GW/390 GWh of battery storage and 44 GW of VRE capacity
(125 GW more solar and 81 GW less wind) is deployed with minimal impact on system costs (less than 0.3% increase
in cost) (Supplementary Figure 18 and Table 17).
In contrast to coal capacity, solar PV, wind, and battery storage technologies dominate new infrastructure investments
across all scenarios under the carbon target, with differences in their levels and mixes driven mainly by their cost
projections. VRE (wind and solar) capacity increases from an existing 72 GW in 2020 to 421 GW in 2030, which
6


## Page 7


meets a large share of India’s official non-fossil or clean energy target of 500 GW. From 2030, VRE capacity more
than triples (3.7 times) to 1,540-1,570 GW by 2040, and then doubles (1.9 times) to 2,890-2,900 GW to achieve a 90%
carbon emissions reduction by 2050. This VRE capacity meets 75% and 92% of energy generation in 2040 and 2050,
respectively. Because of the projected substantial decline in cost of not only solar PV but also battery storage, which
enables the daily balancing of solar energy, solar PV forms an increasing share of VRE installed capacity, from 66-69%
in 2040 to 70-77% in 2050 with higher shares in the low VRE and battery storage cost (VRE & ESS (low) Coal (low))
scenario. Compared to its shares in installed capacity, shares of solar in total VRE energy generation are lower, ranging
from 52-55% in 2040 and 58-64% in 2050, because of the solar PV’s lower capacity factors relative to wind. Within
wind technologies, no offshore wind capacity is built (except in the high demand scenarios, which build only 3.5 GW of
new offshore wind capacity) because of its higher capital costs relative to onshore wind. The system absorbs all VRE
generation in 2030, while curtailment increases to 186 GWh in 2040 and 249 GWh in 2050 (Supplementary Table 18). As
the VRE share of generation increases, the curtailment rate is 4.5% in 2040 and 2050, which is likely an underestimation
because of a lack of representation of transmission line-level congestion in this study.
Energy storage system (ESS) or battery storage, which is limited to short-term (daily) balancing energy in this study,
plays a major role in balancing the diurnal variability of VRE generation, especially solar. In the reference scenario,
battery storage capacity (expressed in both power and energy capacity) rises from near-zero in 2020 to 150 GW/310 GWh
in 2030, 390 GW/790 GWh in 2040, and 650 GW/3,250 GWh in 2050, equivalent to storage purchase obligation (SPO)
levels of 3.3%, 7%, and 26.7% by 2030, 2040, and 2050, respectively (Supplementary Table 18). The high power capacity
relative to energy capacity in 2030 and 2040 is driven by battery storage’s contribution to the planning reserve margin
(PRM), which ensures adequate total system installed capacity to reliably meet demand. This contribution is shown
in Supplementary Figure 17 and Table 16, which compares the battery capacity deployment in the reference scenario
with and without PRM. In 2050, in scenarios with a 90% carbon emissions target, the battery energy capacity increases
substantially—6.6-22 times the energy capacity in 2030—highlighting the greater need for balancing services as the
share of weather-dependent energy sources like wind and solar increases above 90%. The variation in battery energy
capacities in 2050 is driven by technology costs (Figure 3), electricity demand projections (Supplementary Figure 6) and
competition with alternate technologies including green hydrogen storage and pumped storage hydro (Supplementary
Figure 9).
Long-duration green hydrogen storage has the potential to balance energy demand and supply across the whole
year and can help manage the inter-seasonal variability in renewable energy, especially of wind, which generates most
of its electricity in the monsoon months. However, our results show that green hydrogen plays only a limited role in
India’s future low-carbon system. Specifically, hydrogen storage, along with its electrolyzers for hydrogen generation
and fuel cells for electricity generation, is deployed only in 2050 and only in the VRE & ESS mid and high cost scenarios
(Figure 3B). Notably, when clean energy cost projections are low (VRE & ESS low cost scenario), greater capacity of
VRE and battery storage is deployed and no hydrogen storage is built. In the higher clean energy cost scenarios, when
hydrogen storage is deployed, new battery energy capacity requirements decrease because hydrogen replaces some short-
term balancing services provided by battery storage. Even in the VRE & ESS mid and high cost scenarios, while a large
energy capacity of underground hydrogen storage is deployed (1,000 and 2,900 GWh) in 2050, only up to 3.8-11.5 GW of
hydrogen power capacity (both electrolyzer and fuel cell) is installed, representing 0.1-0.3% of total installed generation
and storage power capacity. If green hydrogen is not considered as a candidate technology because of the uncertainty in
its cost, potential, and commercial viability in India, the electricity system relies on greater deployment of both shorter
duration battery storage and VRE to meet the 90% carbon emissions target but with a negligible (below 0.1%) increase
in system cost (Supplementary Figure 14 and Table 13). This limited need for long-duration (annual balancing) storage
is because of the relatively lower inter-seasonal variation in solar generation as well as a substantial correlation between
the monthly combined generation from weather-dependent resources (solar, wind, and hydro) and electricity demand
(Supplementary Figure 25).
We also consider the cost-optimal expansion of two other commercially available technologies—nuclear energy
and pumped storage hydro (PSH)—that could play a role in India’s future low carbon electricity system.
Allow-
ing cost-optimal expansion increases nuclear capacity from 13 GW to 66 GW and PSH from 3.1 GW/19 GWh to
120 GW/880 GWh by 2050. These capacities are below the Government of India’s 100 GW target for nuclear but
above the corresponding target for PSH [24, 25].
Importantly, expansion of PSH and nuclear avoids long-duration
green hydrogen storage and substantially reduces the requirement for battery storage energy capacity (38% lower than
reference) and VRE capacity (8% lower). At the same time, this expansion has a relatively small effect on total costs
in 2050 (less than 2%) (Supplementary Figure 14 and Table 13). We did not include other emerging technologies like
Carbon, Capture, and Storage (CCS) coupled with coal, gas, or biomass power plants, mainly because CCS has not
demonstrated commercial viability in India.

energy target, 90% carbon target by 2050, and mid-growth demand projection with bottom-up profiles. Costs are
shown by technology type for the four periods (2020-2050) across six technology cost scenarios. Fixed costs are shown by solid

2.3
System costs
Technology costs, demand profiles and projections, and clean energy and carbon policies, all drive differences in future
total and average system costs. We first focus on the technology cost scenarios, which result in the largest differences
in system costs. System costs shown here include only generation, storage, and interstate transmission capital and
operational costs and do not include electricity distribution or intrastate transmission and congestion costs.
As electricity demand rises with India’s economic growth, total system costs increase over time, more than doubling
to USD 165-214 billion (INR 11.5-15.0 lakh crores) by 2040 and increasing 2.4-3.6 times to USD 209-315 billion (INR
14.6-22.1 lakh crores) by 2050 (Figure 4A). However, the average system costs across all future investment periods and
technology cost projections including the high-cost scenario (VRE & ESS (high) Coal (high)) remain below the 2020
level of USD 57 per MWh (INR 4.1 per kWh) (Figure 4B).
Solar, wind, and battery storage costs have a substantial effect on total system costs. Compared to the reference
technology cost projections (VRE & ESS (mid) scenarios) under a 90% carbon target by 2050, high VRE and storage
costs (VRE & ESS (high) scenarios) result in 13% and 18% greater system costs in 2040 and 2050 compared to mid-cost
projections, whereas low VRE and storage costs (VRE & ESS (low) scenarios) have 13% and 22% lower system costs,
respectively. In contrast, because of its small share in new capacity investments and generation from new power plants,
coal costs have limited effect on system costs.
If VRE and storage costs follow the low-cost projection (VRE & ESS (low) scenarios), average system costs in 2040
are similar both without and with a 90% carbon target (31% lower in 2040 compared to 2020) but they are even lower
in 2050 when no carbon target is imposed (38% lower compared to 2020 instead of 35% lower under the carbon target).
Yet, the carbon emission reductions without a carbon or clean energy target (32% by 2040 and 46% by 2050 compared
to 2020 emissions, Supplementary Figure 15 and Table 14) fall short of meeting a linear carbon emission reduction
trajectory from 2030 emissions to the 90% target by 2050. Results for all additional scenarios including bottom-up and
linearly-scaled demand projections and alternative technologies (green hydrogen, pumped storage hydro, and nuclear)
are provided in the Supplementary Figures 11–14.
As the share of near-zero variable cost wind and solar resources increases and the share of fossil fuel-based resources
decreases in India’s electricity system, the share of variable costs also commensurately shrinks (Figure 4). Across the
technology cost scenarios, the share of variable costs decreases from 45% in 2020 to 32-36% in 2030, 13-17% in 2040, and
3-4% in 2050. Total variable costs of coal increases from 2020 to 2030 as coal generation increases but then decreases
substantially in 2040 and 2050 as coal generation diminishes. The share of variable costs within just coal’s total costs
falls from 54% in 2030 to 38-39% in 2040 and 8-9% in 2050, highlighting coal plants increasing role in providing capacity
8


## Page 9


for reliability and decreasing energy generation because of unfavorable economics and climate targets.
2.4
State-level generation and interstate electricity trade
The state-level spatial resolution of our model allows examination of the impact of a more distributed build-out of clean
energy across India, reflecting state policies of renewable purchase obligation (RPO) targets (Supplementary Figure 19).
To the 22 largest states by electricity demand (except the city-state of Delhi), we apply a constraint of requiring 50% of
state-wide electricity demand to be met by wind and solar energy deployed within the state boundaries in 2040 and 2050.
Beyond this limit, states with relatively poor renewable resources can import electricity from higher quality out-of-state
renewable energy sites if such imports are cost optimal in spite of costs incurred from interstate transmission losses.
Results show that applying this constraint does not impose any substantial additional costs in 2040 or 2050 (less than
0.1%; see Supplementary Figure 16 and Table 15). The state-level RPO target ensures that deployment of VRE is
relatively spatially balanced across the country except in states like Delhi, Haryana, West Bengal, Bihar, and Kerala,
which either lack high quality VRE resources or border states with high quality renewable resources (Figure 5B,E).
Rajasthan by far has the largest deployment of combined wind and solar capacity, followed by Maharashtra, Gujarat,
Tamil Nadu, Uttar Pradesh, and Karnataka. Northern and eastern states like Uttar Pradesh, Haryana, Punjab, West
Bengal, Odisha, Jharkhand, and Bihar, lack high quality wind resources and rely mainly on solar resources for energy
generation.
Out of the large-demand states, notably, the city-state of Delhi has inadequate land-based renewable
resources and relies on imports for meeting the majority of its electricity demand in all future periods.
The shift from fossil-based energy sources to a much more spatially and temporally heterogeneous solar and wind
resource-based electricity system leads to increased electricity transfers across India’s interstate transmission network
over time (Figure 5A,B and Supplementary Figure 20).
Total electricity transfers increase substantially with each
period, increasing 3–3.1 times by 2040 and 4.3–4.8 times by 2050 (Figure 5C and Supplementary Table 19). Transfers
are especially large coming out of Rajasthan as the state exports its high quality renewable generation to other states.
With increased electricity transfers, requirements for new transmission transfer capacity also increases but only modestly
(26-37% increase in 2050 compared to 2020) (Figure 5D). However, because we represent interstate electricity transfers
using a simplified energy transport model and do not model individual transmission lines in an AC power flow model, we
do not adequately capture the congestion effects on the transmission network, thus underestimating new transmission
requirements.
State-level imports and exports depend on the differences between the state’s electricity demand and generation
potential (Figure 5E) but also the state’s location in the overall network where it could be a conduit for electricity
transfers across regions (see Supplementary Figures 21 and 22). Rajasthan is the largest net exporter of energy because
of its large solar and wind deployment relative to its demand in 2050 (Figure 5F). The state’s exports, 66% of its own in-
state electricity demand, help meet demand in capacity-deficit northern states including Delhi and Haryana. Most other
large western and southern states with the exception of Kerala and Telangana are net exporters (Karnataka, Madhya
Pradesh, Tamil Nadu, and Gujarat) or close to zero net electricity exchanges (Andhra Pradesh and Maharashtra)
(Figure 5F). Most large northern and eastern states including Delhi, Haryana, West Bengal, Uttar Pradesh, Odisha,
Bihar, and Punjab are net importers, mainly because of their relatively poor renewable resources. At the same time,
many states experience substantial imports and exports, much larger than their net transfers, highlighting the importance
of transmission and electricity transfers to balance the diurnal and seasonal variability of solar and wind across India
(see Supplementary Figure 23).
3
Discussion
While there are substantial uncertainties in technology cost and electricity demand projections, examining a wide but
feasible space of input parameters allows us to draw robust conclusions about cost-optimal investments under realistic
constraints, and their impacts on costs and emissions. Here, we discuss insights that are robust to these uncertainties.
3.1
Future system costs decline across technology cost and demand projections while
meeting ambitious carbon reduction targets
India’s relatively low average GDP per capita and development challenges makes it important for the country to prioritize
electricity affordability while balancing its tradeoffs with climate mitigation. Our results show that across all clean and
fossil technology cost and electricity demand projection scenarios, real average system cost i.e., cost of electricity supply,
is lower in each future period (2030-2050) compared to 2020 even with a 90% carbon mitigation target.
In other
words, even with the most pessimistic assumptions about technological advancements driving down wind, solar PV, and
battery storage costs, electricity cost is expected to decline relative to the existing cost (estimated for 2020). Most other
studies have projected declines in real system costs by 2050 [4, 5, 8], while only one has projected an increase over the
