10


## Page 11


same period [12]. This is a critical result that allows India’s policymakers to adopt ambitious climate goals including
clean energy targets without increasing current costs of electricity supply. Realizing these cost reductions makes India’s
renewable energy and storage procurement auctions critical for capturing the gains in future technology cost declines.
Declining technology costs drive the large deployment of solar PV, wind, and battery storage. In fact, in the near-
term (2030), over 787 GW of clean energy or non-fossil capacity is deployed cost-optimally, exceeding the official 500 GW
target, notwithstanding on-the-ground constraints including land availability and transmission interconnections, which
we do not capture in this study. In the medium term (2040), system costs for a scenario with a carbon target of 39%
below 2020 levels (in line with a 90% carbon target by 2050) are similar to one that imposes no carbon target, again
suggesting that declining clean technology costs can make an ambitious carbon target cost-effective. However, not having
a carbon or a clean energy target in 2040 or 2050 results in only about a 20% decline in carbon emissions relative to
2020 across both periods assuming the mid-projection for technology costs (Figure 2C). Even with the low VRE and
storage cost projection, carbon emissions decline to only 46% by 2050 compared to 2020. Thus, to meet a 90% carbon
emissions reduction goal, which is in line with India’s carbon neutrality goal by 2070, climate policies or clean energy
targets are essential.
3.2
Solar, wind, and battery storage dominate new investments while other low-carbon
technologies play a limited role
With its rapidly declining costs and relatively flexible deployment across space and size, solar PV accounts for two-thirds
to three-fourths share of new installed generation capacity across all future periods. Wind power’s higher capacity factors
relative to solar and its improving technology and declining costs makes it the second generation technology to dominate
new investments. To meet cost optimal targets in the reference scenario, deployment rates for solar will need to rise
three times from 28 GW per year in 2025 to 83 GW per year between 2030-2040, and four times to 106 GW per year
between 2040-2050 (Supplementary Figure 3). Deployment rates for wind will need to scale even more from 2024 levels
(4.2 GW per year) by a factor of 6-7 times to 29 GW per year from 2030-2050. In the last 10 years, solar deployment has
scaled up substantially, increasing 9 times from 3.1 GW per year in 2015 to 28 GW per year in 2025, whereas wind power
deployment has been relatively flat (3.4-4.2 GW per year). This reflects greater constraints on wind power development,
which is restricted to limited areas that have high wind resources but also face land and other on-the ground constraints
whereas solar resource is much more evenly distributed across the region, offering more flexibility and choices to project
developers. If wind energy is unable to scale deployment because of on-the-ground constraints, solar deployment may
need to increase beyond the optimal capacity estimated in this study to meet India’s clean energy and climate goals.
In addition to solar and wind, chemical battery storage plays a major role, not only in balancing energy supply and
demand but also in providing reliability services. Because of the detailed reliability modeling in this study, including the
imposition of the planning reserve margin constraint and effective load carrying capacity-based contribution of generation
and storage assets, our results show a relatively higher power and energy capacity requirement compared to other studies
(Supplementary Figure 26). This study considered Lithium Ion-based batteries but other storage technologies with a
different chemistry that can be cost-competitive could also play a role.
Apart from solar PV, wind, and battery storage, all other low carbon technologies considered in this study play a
relatively limited role. Quadrupling the current nuclear capacity to 66 GW or increasing the PSH capacity by 50 times
to 876 GWh has a relatively small impact (less than 2%) on total system costs in 2050 under a 90% carbon emission
reduction target but can avoid substantial deployment of battery storage (38%) and VRE capacity (8%). Despite the
promising potential of long-duration green hydrogen storage in interseasonal energy balancing, it also surprisingly does
not substantially impact total system costs, especially when agricultural and other demand is shifted to day-time solar
hours. This suggests that India should focus on and scale up solar PV, wind, and battery storage deployment in the near
to medium term while exploring opportunities for other low-carbon technology projects that may become cost-effective
over time. For example, green hydrogen could play a major role in decarbonizing India’s industry and sharing this
infrastructure with the power sector may result in cost saving opportunities [11, 26]. Further, emerging low-carbon
technologies like enhanced geothermal energy that were not considered in this study could also play a role in the future
but additional research is needed to assess its cost-competitiveness.
Lastly, technology costs of generation technologies like solar PV, wind, and battery storage are not the only drivers of
electricity costs declines under carbon emission reduction targets. Aligning electricity demand with solar hours through
demand response programs, especially for the agricultural sector, which is part of our reference scenario, substantially
reduces electricity costs (5.4-9.3% lower in the mid to long term) compared to scenarios where demand profiles remain
the same as 2020. This highlights the importance of demand response and energy efficiency programs in limiting cost
increases while ensuring reliability.
11


## Page 12


3.3
Cost-optimal deployment of renewable energy and battery storage in 2040 is three
times the official targets in 2030
India is reasonably on track to meet its official target of 500 GW of non-fossil fuel capacity by 2030, and has a tentative
target of 1,800 GW by 2047 [27]. This study could inform India’s clean energy targets in the medium term (2040).
Under the reference scenario that has a 39% carbon emission reduction target in 2040 relative to 2020 levels, and
assumes medium cost decline trajectories of solar PV, wind, and battery storage, low coal costs, and a climate change-
impacted but solar-aligned future electricity demand, system costs are similar to a scenario in which no clean energy or
carbon targets are applied. Under this scenario, solar and wind capacity in 2040 is 1,080 GW and 480 GW, respectively,
which in total is more than three times the 2030 official target of 450 GW. Solar and wind generate 75% of 2040
electricity demand, whereas renewable energy share (which also includes biomass, hydropower, and WHR) increases to
81% and total clean energy share, which includes nuclear, rises to 86%. Power and energy capacities of battery storage
are 390 GW and 790 GWh, which are approximately three times the capacity targets in 2030. Our cost-optimal solar
PV, wind, and battery storage capacities in 2040 remain within the relatively broad ranges reported in prior studies of
India’s power system transition (380–1,700 GW for solar PV, 340–760 GW for wind, and 130–390 GW/540–1,800 GWh
for battery storage), see Supplementary Figure 26.
While clean energy capacity targets can serve as a guiding benchmark for national energy planning, what drives
procurement in India is the state-level renewable energy and energy storage portfolio obligations (RPO and ESO),
which are shares of renewable energy and battery discharge energy as a percentage of energy demand [28, 29]. Although
the southern and western states have substantially greater wind resources compared to northern and eastern states, the
relative similarity in solar resource quality across India and low costs of solar PV allows all states with the exception
of Delhi to meet at least half of their renewable energy demand from in-state resources across all periods with similar
system costs compared to a scenario that imposes no requirement for in-state deployment. This suggests that most states
and their distribution utilities can pursue their own procurement and deployment strategies to meet their obligation
targets without significantly affecting costs.
3.4
Changing cost structures, interest rates, and decreasing capacity factors of coal
impact markets and contracts
Compared to India’s coal-dominated electricity system in 2020, the system cost of India’s future electricity system is
increasingly dominated by fixed costs as technologies like solar, wind, and battery storage, which have high upfront
costs and low variable costs, are increasingly deployed (Figure 4). Relying on these technologies can help reduce the
electricity sector’s exposure to future inflation in fuel costs. However, high interest rates can have a substantial negative
impact on the deployment of low-carbon technologies because of the greater capital required for investment. In addition,
technologies with low variable costs will increasingly drive marginal prices toward zero, undermining investment recovery
in India’s electricity market and requiring market mechanisms to value capacity, flexibility, and reliability [30].
Increasing decarbonization of India’s electricity system decreases not only the deployment of fossil fuel generators
but also their operation or capacity factors, which can affect their financial viability. Our results show that over time
but within the lifetimes of power plants, capacity factors of fuel-based generators, especially coal, decrease substantially
as solar and wind energy with near-zero marginal costs displace higher marginal cost fuel-based energy generation (Sup-
plementary Figure 24). Yet, coal plants are needed to meet the primary reserve margin and provide reliability services.
This results in an increasing share of fixed costs and a decreasing share of variable costs in total coal energy generation
costs. Furthermore, coal plants are increasingly cycled to balance the variability in renewable energy generation and
electricity demand, which leads to increasing equipment wear and tear, thus incurring additional maintenance costs
[31, 32]. For coal plants to remain economically viable for reliability and balancing purposes, their increasing costs per
unit of electricity generation may need to be increasingly recovered from the fixed component of their compensation or
through the ancillary services markets, necessitating changes in contracts and electricity markets. However, we caution
that variable cost components in long-term contracts that do not reflect the true marginal costs of coal power plants
can distort merit-order dispatch, leading to those coal plants to which such contracts apply being dispatched ahead of
lower-cost generators and thereby increasing total system costs and carbon emissions.
3.5
Detailed studies required for transmission planning, operations, and pricing
While our study shows substantial increase in interstate electricity trade (4.3–4.8 times in 2050 compared to 2020 levels),
we record only a modest increase in interstate transmission transfer capacity. This result is driven by the coarse spatial
resolution of our model, which does not capture the power flow constraints on transmission lines, both within and
between states. To adequately inform transmission planning, capacity expansion and production cost models need to be
coupled closely with transmission models that can simulate power flows in future electricity system scenarios and build
new transmission capacity based on congestion. Voltage and frequency stability studies and “n-1” contingency studies,
12


## Page 13


which examine the effect of a transmission line or generator failure on the rest of the system, will be needed to assess
future transmission plans and operations [33]. Lastly, improvements in transmission pricing and allocation are essential
for optimal transmission utilization and providing appropriate signals for new investments [34].
3.6
Other considerations and future research
While our study shows that India’s power sector can deeply decarbonize with no additional system costs compared
to 2020 levels, more research is needed on technical, environmental, and social impacts of India’s future decarbonized
system. We discuss some of these research considerations below.
Ensuring reliability in a future decarbonized power system, especially one that will rely mostly on weather-dependent
renewable resources, requires a robust analysis of resource adequacy. This study considers only a single weather year
in the capacity expansion model but relies on capacity credits derived from 18 years of weather data and the planning
reserve margin constraint to identify adequate cost-optimal generation, storage, and transmission capacity that meets
future demand. Designing and operating future scenarios of India’s electricity system under multiple weather years to
account for interannual variability in generation and demand, incorporating the effects of climate change on generation,
transmission, and demand, estimating reliability metrics like unserved energy, and examining the effects of extreme
events will improve the robustness of the results.
Decarbonized electricity systems that predominantly rely on solar and wind resources have low system synchronous
inertia (Supplementary Figure 27 and Table 20), which is a major concern for system operators [35]. Typically, systems
with high shares of thermal generators with rotating turbines have significant synchronous inertia that slows the rate
of change of frequency and help stabilize the system immediately following a disturbance event [36]. In contrast, solar
PV, wind, and battery storage technologies are inverter-based, low-inertia systems, and although wind turbines have
some limited inertia in their rotors, their power electronic interfaces decouple these rotating masses from grid frequency.
Thus, highly decarbonized systems that are dominated by these technologies do not have this ability to resist frequency
changes because of a lack of significant rotating mass in the system [37]. Although the inverter-based technologies could
provide synthetic inertia [38], the potential and costs of mandating such a service needs to be evaluated.
Both solar and wind can have land footprints larger than coal and natural gas infrastructure [39]. Making coarse
assumptions for land use factors of 29 and 43 MW/km2 for solar PV (single-axis and fixed tilt respectively) and
4.4 MW/km2 for wind [40], land requirements for solar and wind in the reference scenario would be 143,000 km2 for
1,570 GW in 2040 (the size of Chhattisgarh) and 233,000 km2 for 2,890 GW in 2050 (the size of Gujarat). These
land requirements will result in substantial trade-offs against other land uses like agriculture and conservation. While
previous studies have shown that adequate potential for both solar and wind exist on suitable low-conflict lands [18, 41,
42], studies have found actual deployment on prime agricultural land, which is in direct conflict with food production
[43]. Similarly, solar PV power plants could have adverse local environmental impacts, especially if sited in water-scarce
regions [44, 45]. More research on existing deployment patterns, potential low-conflict land use pathways, and low water
consumption practices for solar technologies could enable planners to set policies and rules that avoid adverse effects of
renewable energy siting.
Renewable energy and battery storage manufacturing and deployment can potentially create more jobs than India’s
coal industry that it aims to replace. Pai et al. [46] show that total energy sector jobs in India are projected to grow
under most climate policy and socioeconomic assumptions, but their analysis is limited to direct employment effects
[47]. A more comprehensive study examining the impacts of decarbonization pathways on not just direct jobs and wage
compensation, but also the indirect effects of employment on the rest of the economy is essential.
This study shows that India can substantially decarbonize its electricity sector by 2050 without increasing system
costs. But several challenges including deployment scale-up, transmission planning, land use tradeoffs, and employment
shifts, need to be addressed to meet ambitious carbon mitigation targets.
4
Methods
To estimate electricity infrastructure investments, costs, and emissions of India’s future electricity system, we developed
an open-source capacity expansion and production cost model using GridPath, an open-source modeling platform.
This model co-optimizes investments and operations of generators, storage, and transmission across four investment
periods—2020, 2030, 2040, and 2050—at 35 state-level demand nodes, subject to several technical, economic, and policy
constraints. Below, we first describe the input data and then the model.
4.1
Generators
Existing generators.
Existing generator capacity is from the fiscal year 2019-2020 (FY20).
We aggregate this
capacity by vintage (commission year), state, and technology type for model tractability. Technology types include
13


## Page 14


supercritical coal (unit capacity ≥600 MW), subcritical coal large (unit capacity ≥250 MW ≥600 MW), subcritical coal
small (unit capacity <250 MW), combined cycle gas turbine (CCGT) and combustion turbine (CT) for natural gas,
diesel, biomass (including bagasse or sugarcane-based co-generation units), waste heat recovery (WHR), hydro storage,
hydro run-of-the-river (ROR), pumped storage hydropower (PSH), onshore and offshore wind, fixed tilt, single axis,
and rooftop solar photovoltaic (PV), battery storage, and hydrogen storage. See Supplementary Table 27 for state-wise
capacity for each technology type. Each coal type is associated with a different heat rate (Supplementary Table 23).
Local and imported liquefied natural gas have different costs and states that host natural gas generators use one or the
other based on historical usage (Supplementary Table 36). Hydro storage projects balance energy across each month
with a fixed monthly energy budget based on historical generation. Hydro ROR is treated as must run, generating at
historical average daily generation levels (Supplementary Note 3). Hydro ROR is also the only technology considered
in the country of Bhutan, which exports electricity to India. Existing PSH fleet has an average duration of 6 h. All
existing wind generators are onshore and assumed to be at 80 m hub height. We assume all existing solar capacity is
fixed tilt even though small percentages of single axis and rooftop PV were deployed by 2020 [48, 49]. Existing nuclear
capacity is in only six states (Supplementary Table 27).
Existing coal generators are retired exogenously based on age (Supplementary Table 36); e.g., coal power plants
are retired after a 45-year operational lifetime [50]. Because of their relatively low installed capacity, existing projects
of all other technologies are assumed to remain online through the entire study period. All new projects are retired
endogenously after their operational lifetime (Supplementary Table 35).
Power plant unit-level information about
vintages of existing generators is from state-level tariff orders and the MERIT database [51] detailed in Supplementary
Note 4.
New generators.
We include hydro ROR (13.3 GW), supercritical coal (36.5 GW) [52], and nuclear (4.8 GW)
projects [53] that are in the pipeline, i.e., commissioned after 2020 or currently under construction. See projects in the
pipeline by state in Supplementary Table 24. In addition to projects in the pipeline, we consider new candidate projects
for different technologies and locations for the model to cost-optimally select (Supplementary Table 22). We allow
candidate supercritical coal projects in 17 states, which have high electricity demand or existing coal capacity. We allow
new gas projects (CCGT and CT) only in 12 states with existing natural gas generation capacity (except in Assam)
and allow the expansion of only imported liquefied natural gas because of constraints on domestic supplies of natural
gas [54]. Beyond projects in the pipeline, nuclear capacity is allowed to expand cost-optimally in certain scenarios in
states with existing capacity. Candidate PSH projects have a total power capacity of 138 GW and an energy capacity
of 839 GWh (average storage duration of 6 h) based on projects recommended for environmental impact assessment
by India’s Expert Appraisal Committee [25]. New battery storage projects are considered in 21 states with existing
coal or natural gas projects. For long duration storage (balancing energy via charge-discharge cycles across an entire
year), we consider green hydrogen, which is generated from water via electrolysis with a proton exchange membrane
(PEM) electrolyzer [55]. Hydrogen can be liquefied and stored in tanks or non-porous caverns, and is then oxidized in
fuel cells to generate electricity and water. We consider underground nonporous cavern storage in states where suitable
formations are available (Supplementary Table 22), and do not place limits on the energy capacity of those sites [56].
For states where underground cavern storage is unavailable, we consider hydrogen storage projects with tank storage,
which are more expensive but more flexible in siting.
Operational characteristics.
Subcritical and supercritical coal, natural gas CCGT and CT, and diesel generators
are dispatchable with constraints on minimum up and down time hours, and minimum stable level fraction considering
unit sizes. Nuclear generators are dispatchable but relatively inflexible with a 90% minimum generation level. Hydro
storage projects are must-run generators constrained by average monthly capacity factors across monthly balancing
horizons based on historical generation data and by their rated capacity and minimum historical generation level for
maximum and minimum instantaneous generation. PSH and battery storage have daily balancing horizons with the
same state-of-charge at the end of the daily balancing cycle. Biomass, hydro ROR, solar PV, and wind generators
are non-dispatchable and hourly dispatch is based on historical generation. Biomass and hydro ROR are considered
not curtailable, while solar PV and wind are treated as curtailable generation sources. Operational characteristics by
technology are given in Supplementary Table 35.
Monthly capacity factors for hydro ROR and storage are the median values from annual generation reports published
by the CEA in fiscal year 2019, 2020 and 2021 (April 2018 - March 2021) [57]. These reports provide monthly generation
for 94 hydro ROR and 84 hydro storage generators with their nameplate capacity. We aggregate the energy generation
across load zones to calculate monthly capacity factors for hydro ROR and storage at the state level. Hydro storage
projects are dispatchable and have a monthly energy budget, constrained by a monthly average capacity factor. Hydro
ROR generators are non-dispatchable and must-run with hourly capacity factors set equal to monthly average capacity
factors. Capacity factors for hydro ROR in Bhutan are linearly adjusted to match exports (6.35 TWh) in FY20 [22].
We consider biomass energy generators (including those operating with agricultural residues and sugarcane bagasse)
14


## Page 15


as must-run with historical monthly average capacity factors from FY2020 assigned to hourly capacity factors in the
model [58].
For fuel-burning technology types, heat rates, which is the amount of fuel energy (in MMBtu) required to generate
1 MWh of electricity, and CO2 emission rates in CO2 tons per MMBtu are provided in Supplementary Table 23.
4.2
Wind and solar resources
For identifying candidate onshore wind and solar sites, we adopted the Multi-criteria Analysis for Planning Renewable
Energy (MapRE) modeling framework [59] and the REZoning platform for candidate offshore wind sites [60]. MapRE
is a spatial energy systems modeling framework that integrates renewable resource assessment, estimation of multiple
criteria for decision-making analysis, and site selection [59]. REZoning is the global scale version of MapRE, including
offshore wind siting capabilities (Supplementary Note 1).
We identify multiple suitable candidate sites at the state level by considering social (e.g., population, UNESCO
sites), infrastructural (e.g., roads, ports, and water access), and environmental (e.g., protected area, terrain, slope)
constraints to determine the available capacity at each site for renewable energy deployment. We select 556 solar PV
sites, 300 onshore wind sites, and 46 offshore sites with suitable solar (global tilt irradiance ≥4 kWh/m2 per day) or
wind resources (wind speed at 100 m ≥5 m/s). To represent both utility-scale solar PV technologies, we assign 80%
of potential capacity at each candidate site to single-axis tracking systems and 20% to fixed-tilt systems, totaling 1,112
solar PV sites. For representing rooftop solar PV deployment, we add a candidate project site within each of the 35
load zones within India with solar generation profiles from the largest city within each state.
We simulate the capacity factor time series for each solar and wind candidate site, using European Centre for
Medium-Range Weather Forecasts (ECMWF) ReAnalysis 5th Generation (ERA5) for wind, and National Solar Ra-
diation Database (NSRDB) from the National Laboratory of the Rockies (formerly the National Renewable Energy
Laboratory) for solar [61, 62]. The model in the NSRDB with coverage for India is the PSM V3, which has a high
spatial resolution (4 km), while ERA5 has a relatively low spatial resolution (31 km). Both models have hourly temporal
resolution. Since PSM V3 extends only until 2019, we use ERA5 and PSM V3 for the Fiscal Year 2018-19 (FY19).
To overcome the limitation of low resolution of wind resource (onshore and offshore), we adjust the wind velocity time
series data from ERA5 for each location such that the annual averages match the average wind speeds from the nearest
locations in the Global Wind Atlas (GWA) [63]. GWA data have a higher spatial resolution and are considered more
accurate than ERA5. Simulated generation from existing solar PV sites matched the reported generation in FY19, but
the simulated generation from wind sites is higher. To reconcile this difference, we adjust the existing annual average
capacity factors to match FY19 wind generation aggregated at the state level as explained in Supplementary Note 3.
To convert hourly solar radiation to capacity factors using PVWatts [64], we assume new solar PV fixed-tilt projects to
have standard monocrystalline modules, and single-axis tracking systems to use high-efficiency monocrystalline modules.
Rooftop solar PV systems also have standard monocrystalline modules, but with higher losses due to soiling [65]. New
wind projects consider 120 m hub height turbines (2 MW), and offshore wind projects 135 m hub height turbines (7 MW).
Class I, II, and III turbines are assigned to onshore wind sites based on high, mid, and low wind speeds, respectively,
whereas all offshore wind sites are assigned the Vestas 164 turbine (Supplementary Note 3).
4.3
Technology costs
Annualized capital investment plus fixed operation and maintenance (O&M) costs along with the fuel and variable
O&M costs for existing thermal generators (coal, gas, and diesel) are from tariff orders of 12 states for the FY18 or
FY19 [66–80]. Project-level data not available in tariff orders were collected from the “Merit Order of Despatch for the
Rejuvenation of Income and Transparency (MERIT)” program in 2020 [51].
The investment, fixed, and variable O&M costs for hydroelectric generators (hydro ROR, storage, and PSH tech-
nologies), nuclear, and biomass/bagasse are not available in tariff orders or the MERIT program. Thus, we adopt the
costs from the 2022 Indian Technology Catalogue (ITC) [81] as a baseline and compare them with alternative esti-
mates reported in the literature to construct consistent India-specific low, medium, and high cost projection scenarios
(Supplementary Note 4).
The cost projections for solar PV and wind energy integrate data from multiple sources (Supplementary Note 4). For
solar PV with single-axis tracking, we linearly adjust the 2023 Annual Technology Baseline (ATB) [82] estimates using
IRENA’s 2020 auction prices in India [83] and align them with the long-term estimates in the 2022 ITC. We develop
fixed-tilt solar PV projections by linearly scaling our India-specific utility-scale PV single-axis estimates using a scale
factor from the literature [84]. The solar PV roof-mount cost projections are from medium- and high-cost scenarios for
the individual components under the 2022 ITC. The initial costs in 2020 are from the upper scenario. However, we
linearly adjusted each component cost projection using a scale factor estimated from commercial and residential modules
in the U.S. [85]. The mid-cost scenario is the average of the new high and low cost scenarios. For onshore wind, we use
15


## Page 16


the 2020 ATB initial cost and retain the original 2023 ATB projections from 2030 onward, but linearly adjusted using
IRENA’s auction data in India. Offshore wind projections are from the 2023 ATB, but linearly adjusted using a scale
factor estimated from U.S. (2023 ATB) and India (2022 ITC) onshore wind prices, given the absence of IRENA auction
data for India. However, the 2022 ITC is still the lower bound, and the adjusted 2023 ATB is the upper bound. The
middle scenario is the average between the two.
Similarly, we derive PSH, battery, and hydrogen storage cost estimates by combining information from multiple
sources and refining these projections based on regional and technological considerations (Supplementary Note 4). PSH
cost projections are from the 2024 ITC Power Storage [86]. However, decompose PSH power and energy components
based on U.S. data [87], and apply a scale factor to the estimates based on recent project costs [88]. Battery cost
projections are from the 2023 ATB [89].
We linearly adjust these projections with a scale factor estimated from
international auctions and ongoing tenders [90], and decompose the power and energy components using U.S. data. Due
to significant discrepancies between projected and actual auction costs, we further linearly adjust the power [83], and
the energy component [91] separately. Hydrogen cost projections assume production via proton exchange membrane
electrolysis, storage in tanks or caverns, and electricity generation through fuel cells.
Under the assumption that
hydrogen combustion turbines currently cost less than fuel cells, but fuel cells will cost less than combustion turbines by
2030 [92]. Cost projections integrate data from 2024 ITC Green Fuels [93], and international studies, but are adjusted
to account for uncertainty and aligned with local auction prices. Given the lack of local data, salt cavern storage cost
estimates rely on international studies [87, 94, 95], while considering regional salt cavern availability [56]. The fuel cell
projections combine the estimations from multiple studies in the literature to ensure consistency in long-term cost trends
[96]. However, under these cost assumptions, the model does not select hydrogen storage in those states, and therefore
limited hydrogen projects to regions with access to underground storage to reduce the model complexity (Supplementary
Table 22).
New coal (supercritical), Combustion Turbine (CT), and CCGT projects are mature technologies and thus, we
assume constant real cost projections from 2020 to 2050. Coal costs reflect recent economic proposals, with mid and
high estimates capturing construction and procurement uncertainties. CCGT and CT costs are from the 2022 ITC. The
costs for CCGT in the 2022 ITC are lower than the costs for CT. We use the high cost scenario in the CT estimations
for CCGT and the mid cost for CT. These costs are similar with the assumptions in recent studies [8, 97]. Nuclear
costs are from the 2022 ITC. We assume stable costs from 2020 to 2050. We use a piecewise cubic hermite interpolation
polynomial to smooth the cost projections for all technologies (Supplementary Note 4).
Variable O&M costs are from international projections, 2023 ATB, 2022 ITC, 2024 ITC Power Storage, and 2024 ITC
Green Fuels, and we only assume mid-cost estimates across all scenarios. We model the costs for emerging technologies
(hydrogen storage, solar PV, and onshore wind) to decline slightly through 2050, except for offshore wind and battery
energy components, which see a significant decrease, and battery power components remain steady. Mature technologies
(coal and nuclear) experience increasing variable O&M costs, while CT, CCGT, and PSH remain stable across the
study period. Fuel costs for new thermal generators are from the specific capacity data detailed in Supplementary
Note 4. We assume international liquefied gas prices for new CT and CCGT generators. Capital investment costs are
annualized with fixed O&M costs added for each technology. Fuel and variable O&M costs are combined for conventional
technologies. Storage efficiencies and project lifetimes reflect current estimates without future improvements, as found
in Supplementary Note 4.
We use a 7% social discount rate to compute electricity system’s net present value (NPV) from a central planning
perspective, consistent with macroeconomic benchmarks such as the Reserve Bank of India’s FY20 policy rate. To
annualize overnight capital costs, we apply a 9% financing rate reflecting new projects weighted average cost of capital
(WACC) which is within the range (8.8%-10% post-tax) in India’s power sector [98]. The financing rate is higher because
it reflects the private cost of capital required to attract investment, including risk premia and equity return requirements,
whereas the social discount rate represents a planner’s intertemporal valuation of system costs and does not embed risk
specific to a project.
We use average exchange rates from 2020 to preserve the infrastructure expansion investment’s NPV in 2020 USD.
The EUR to USD exchange rate is 1.142, and the USD to IND is 72.
4.4
Demand projections
To explore the uncertainty in future demand projections, we develop two sets of demand profiles, a bottom-up set and
a linearly-scaled set. Within each set, we use three different demand growth projections.
To create the bottom-up demand projection profiles, we use PIER v2.0, an energy systems model that develops
state-wise electricity demand projections for India [19], with hourly granularity for a representative (average) day for
each month. This model captures sectoral energy use by integrating bottom-up energy service-based methods, top-down
GDP elasticity projections, and trend analysis. A bottom-up estimation approach models residential, transport, and
industrial demand. This approach considers consumer types with different energy needs (consumption patterns) and
16


## Page 17


different technological adoption and efficiencies, which allows PIER v2.0 to model energy demand from residential,
industrial, and transport sectors in detail. The remaining demand is projected using historical trends and calibrated
with reported consumption data [99].
To develop hourly demand profiles for an entire year, we use electricity demand from Fiscal Year 2019 (FY19),
reported by Grid-India, the electricity system operator [100], and NITI Aayog, the Indian government think tank [101].
We select FY19 profiles to preserve the weather synchronization with the capacity. These datasets, combined with
PIER v2.0 projections (Supplementary Note 5), provide the foundation for developing sector-specific demand profiles
for agriculture, industry, and commercial sectors [19]. While demand profiles from industrial and commercial sectors
are projected to remain relatively stable, the agricultural sector presents a key transformation: a shift toward daytime
electricity consumption driven by the “solarization” of agricultural power supply. This shift reflects a policy-driven
intervention to reshape when and how energy is consumed.
For demand growth projections, we adapt three contrasting demand scenarios developed by PIER v2.0: “Reference”
(mid), “Vikasit Bharat” (high), and “Vichalit Bharat” (low) [19]. The “Reference” scenario follows historical trends,
current targets, and expected sectoral shifts. “Vikasit Bharat” envisions high household incomes, effective policies, and
sustainable lifestyles by 2047, leading to higher but more efficient energy consumption. In contrast, “Vichalit Bharat”
represents slower economic growth, a chaotic policy-making landscape, and unsustainable practices, resulting in lower
but less efficient energy demand.
These scenarios provide a comprehensive framework for analyzing India’s future
electricity demand patterns under different socio-economic conditions.
To assess the effect of demand profiles that remain similar to current profiles, we use the same publicly available
state-level hourly demand from FY19 [100]. We then linearly scale the hourly demand profiles to match total demand
projections independently for each state and month from PIER v2.0. Next, we linearly scale the peak demand to match
the monthly peak demand without changing the total state-level monthly demand. The proposed method to generate
the demand profiles is further explained in Supplementary Note 6.
4.5
Electricity system model
We use GridPath, an open-source production cost and capacity expansion modeling platform [17], which defines a
Mixed-Integer Linear programming (MILP) problem, written in Python using the optimization package Pyomo [102],
and calculates the solution with a commercial solver Gurobi [103]; see formulation in Supplementary Note 2. We simulate
the India’s electricity system model (GridPath-India) over four investment periods—2020, 2030, 2040, and 2050. The
model has a spatial resolution of 35 load zones where each load zone is associated with an hourly electricity demand
profile and existing and candidate generator and storage projects. Load zones are connected by transmission corridors
following existing transmission network connections. In the capacity expansion model, each investment period has an
hourly temporal resolution but only two representative days for each month for maintaining model tractability. The
model co-optimizes hourly operating costs and investments (over four fiscal years) in new system infrastructure including
generation, storage, and transmission, while meeting hourly electricity demand and meeting reliability and policy goals.
We then run the hourly operations model over an entire investment period’s 8,760 hours by fixing the new capacity
investments in that period to ensure system reliability.
The inputs to GridPath include projected hourly electricity demand for each investment period, existing and can-
didate generator capacities, hourly capacity factors for weather-dependent generators (biomass, wind, solar, and hydro
ROR), monthly hydropower budgets (hydro storage), candidate generator capacities in the pipeline, and transmission
infrastructure capacities. The required techno-economic parameters include generators’ fixed O&M costs, variable O&M
costs (which include fuel costs in this project), unit investment costs of expanding the transmission lines, heat rates,
capital costs per technology, lifetime, and CO2 emission rates. The proposed policy parameters include capacity group
requirements for specifying clean energy deployment targets, CO2 emission limits (i.e., carbon caps), and renewable
energy purchase obligations (share of electricity demand to be met by renewable energy). The system reliability pa-
rameters include planning reserve margin (additional available generation or storage capacity above peak demand as
a percentage of peak demand), generators’ availability factors, firm and variable generators’ capacity credits (available
capacity during peak demand), and projections (state and regional level) of hourly regulation reserves and spinning
reserve provisions.
The outputs from GridPath are new generator, storage, and transmission project capacities by investment period,
as well as hourly electricity dispatch, curtailment, unserved energy, and transmission losses, exports and imports among
the states, variable O&M costs, reserve provisions, and CO2 emissions.
Temporal structure.
We have 8,760 hourly profiles for the fiscal years FY20, FY30, FY40, and FY50, and we
represent them as the 2020, 2030, 2040, and 2050 investment periods. In the capacity expansion model, we reduce the
number of timepoints to produce a computationally tractable solution while maintaining representativeness. For that,
17


## Page 18


we select two representative days for each of the 12 months in an investment period to preserve seasonal and diurnal
weather variability patterns.
We select the median day per month to capture seasonal trends and the peak demand day per month to capture
extreme regional characteristics. Therefore, we select two representative days per month and assign them a different
weight (τ) in the optimization function. The weight for peak day is τ = 1 and weight for median day is τ = Nm −1,
where Nm is the number of days (N) in the month (m = {1, . . . , 12}).
We have 4 periods with 4 × 2 × 12 = 96
representative days and 96 × 24 = 2304 total time points.
We independently select days for each investment period in the mid bottom-up and linearly-scaled demand scenarios
(see Supplementary Note 6). These same selected days for each investment period in a demand scenario (bottom-up
and linearly-scaled) are used in the low and high demand scenarios.
Load zones.
The electricity transmission network in India (i.e., the national grid) is formed by five interconnected
regional power grids: Northern, Eastern, Western, Northeastern, and Southern. The national grid has a synchronous
interconnection with the neighboring country of Bhutan. The “National Load Despatch Centre” and the “Regional Load
Despatch Centres” coordinate the operations at the national and regional levels, respectively. The “State Load Despatch
Centres” balance the grids at the state and union territory level. We model 35 load zones where each state and union
territory that is part of contiguous India is considered a separate load zone with the following exceptions (Supplementary
Table 21). Union territories of Jammu and Kashmir and Ladakh are combined into a single zone. Dadra Nagar Haveli
and Diu and Daman are administratively a single union territory but because they are geographically discontinuous,
they are treated as separate nodes in this model. In addition, we include the country of Bhutan as an energy exporting
load zone but assume its own electricity demand to be zero in the model to separate energy exporting generators from
generators meeting domestic demand.
Transmission lines.
Load zones are connected by interstate transmission corridors with limits on transmission trans-
fer capacities (Table 28). We adopt existing transmission transfer capacity data compiled from India’s national system
operator, Grid-India, by a previous study [104]. The geographical coordinates assigned to each load zone correspond
to the most populated city in the state. The transmission losses in existing and new transmission corridors depend on
the distance between the two interconnected load zones, and it is proportional to the energy transfer. We define our
transmissions loss factor as 0.016 %/km.
The new transmission capacity costs are divided into line and substation costs. The average transmission line cost
is 0.96 USD/kW-km, and the average substation cost is 22.5 USD/kW in India. We assume 50 years as the average
transmission line lifetime. In addition, we exogenously add existing transmission capacity cost of USD 5.79 Billion,
reported by the Central Electricity Regulatory Commission for FY22 in their annual report on “Short-Term Power
Market in India” [105].
The “General Review” annually published by CEA contains information about electricity theft in transmission losses
[106]. We estimate total electricity theft using the simulated transmission losses and the transmission losses reported
by CEA (Supplementary Table 25). The transmission losses report shows a steady decrease in losses in the last 10 fiscal
years. We projected electricity theft and included demand projections (Supplementary Table 26).
Resource adequacy and operational reliability.
To account for bias in the investment portfolio for using repre-
sentative days in the capacity expansion model and to address interannual variability in generation resources, we include
a Planning Reserve Margin (PRM) constraint, which is the additional capacity above peak demand required to maintain
a reliable electricity supply [107]. We assume a PRM level of 8%.
Each generator and storage asset contributes to the PRM a capacity equivalent to its Efficiency Load-Carrying
Capacity (ELCC). The percentage of its rated capacity that the ELCC represents is its capacity credit. The capacity
credits for dispatchable generators and energy storage are their average historical availability factors. The capacity
credit of a VRE generator is the percentage of its capacity that contributes to supplying the peak electricity demand
[108]. Following the “Top Net Load Hours” methodology outlined by CEA for estimating capacity credits [108], we use
hourly capacity factors for each VRE asset and weather-synchronized hourly demand aggregated at the national level
to calculate net demand over 18 years (see Supplementary Note 7). We generate national hourly demand for each of the
18 weather years by weather-synchronizing demand using two historical years of state-level hourly demand data. We
then sort the capacity factors for each asset in ascending order of hourly net demand and average the capacity factors
corresponding to the top 5% hours with the highest net demand for each asset to estimate their capacity credits.
For availability factors for each technology and state, we use average monthly reports of historical capacity factors
and outage rates from FY19 to FY25 [101]. The proposed availability factors account for limitations in fuel availability
to differentiate between states that consume local gas or coal and those that import them. We use the capacity factors
and the outage rates to derive the fuel availability (Supplementary Note 9)
18


## Page 19


To ensure operational reliability, we apply the operational reserve constraint at the state and regional levels as
specified by the national system operator, Grid-India. There are three levels of operational reserves [109]. Primary
reserves are provided by online generators and are automatically dispatched (less than 1 sec) for grid stability. Secondary
reserves are spinning reserves provided by a fraction of the committed generation and must respond quickly to imbalances
between demand and supply (1 to 5 min). Tertiary reserves are non-spinning reserves for frequency regulation, activated
to restore the secondary reserves and must respond in 5 to 15 min. We model secondary reserves as Spinning Reserves
(SR) and tertiary reserves as Frequency Response (FR) at the state and regional levels in the GridPath model. We
aggregate the reserve requirements at the regional level to limit the number of constraints. We do not model primary
reserves because they are provided by running generators, and their response is faster than the temporal resolution
of our model. To determine the reserve levels, we follow the method outlined in the “Detailed Procedure on Interim
Methodology for Estimation of Reserves Under CERC (Ancillary Services) Regulations 2022” [110]. We project the
reserve requirements to future investment periods based on the secondary reserves and tertiary reserves requirements
from FY24, Quarter 2, assuming that the ratios between the peak demand and reserves, and the largest unit size, remain
be the same (see Supplementary Note 8).
To ensure reliable operation of the electricity system, we run a production cost operational model for 8,760 hours of
the entire year after fixing the new generator, storage, and transmission investments in each investment period.
Policy goals.
We include three different clean energy and climate policy goals. We impose India’s official goal of 500
GW of clean-energy capacity by 2030 (i.e., clean target). Clean-energy includes VRE, large hydropower, and nuclear
projects. We enforce the clean target as system level upper bound constraint because of the favorable economics of solar
and wind technologies. We also enforce India’s official renewable portfolio obligation (RPO) target that sets a minimum
share of VRE generation to geographically balance VRE deployment across investment periods. Official annual RPO
targets are specified from 2025 to 2030. We extend these targets exogenously to 2040 and 2050, reaching approximately
50% of total generation by 2040 and holding this level constant until 2050. As national policy, “hilly” states target is
half of the official RPO. To reduce the computational load, we enforce the RPO as lower bound constraint only in the 21
states with high demand (Supplementary Table 22), which captures the majority of the national electricity consumption.
Finally, we impose a national-level linearly decreasing annual carbon emissions (i.e., carbon) target that enforces a 80%,
90%, and 100% reduction by 2050 compared to carbon emissions in 2020.
Data Availability
GridPath-India is publicly available in the Dryad repository [111]. The repository contains the GridPath input files for
the scenarios analyzed in this investigation, including transmission and project portfolios, operational characteristics,
reliability requirements, policy targets, effective load-carrying capability assumptions for variable renewable energy
projects, availability factors, and temporal structures necessary to replicate the results. The repository also includes
project-level wind and solar capacity factor profiles for multiple technology configurations, along with the India-specific
technology cost projections used in this study.
Code Availability
The solar and wind site suitability analysis was developed using MapRE, publicly available at https://github.com/
cetlab-ucsb/mapre. The GridPath open-source power system modeling platform is available at https://github.com/
blue-marble/gridpath. The visualization tools developed to generate the figures in research are publicly available in
a GitHub repository at https://github.com/cetlab-ucsb/gridpath_india_viz.

Figure 1: GridPath-India Modeling Framework. GridPath-India is open-source India national model built on the GridPath
power system planning platform. It determines cost-optimal investments in generation, storage, and transmission assets under
technical, economic, and policy constraints, co-optimizing both new investments and operations of the electricity system across
multiple planning periods. It also optimizes hourly or sub-hourly operations of the electricity system to evaluate reliability.
Figure 2:
Existing, planned capacity, and coal retirements in GridPath-India.
Power capacity aggregated at the
national level and grouped by resource. Coal includes supercritical and subcritical (large and small units) technologies, while
hydro includes run-of-the-river (ROR) and pumped storage (PSH) technologies. Others include biomass and waste heat recovery.

Table 3: Technology cost, policy, and demand scenarios. The technology cost scenarios consider low/mid/high variable renewable energy (VRE) and energy storage
system (ESS) costs, and low/high supercritical coal. The policy scenarios include the enacted policies of a 500 MW clean-energy generation capacity target by 2030 and
the state-wise renewable purchase obligation (RPO) projected to 2050, and explore 80%/90%/100% CO2 emission reduction targets from 2020 levels by 2050. The demand
scenarios consider low/mid/high demand scenarios from bottom-up or linearly-scaled demand profiles.

## Page 66


Supplementary Notes
1
Solar PV and Wind Zones
The potential VRE zones were selected using the Multi-criteria analysis for planning Renewable Energy (MapRE).
MapRE was developed at the Lawrence Berkeley National Laboratory (LBNL) and is maintained at the University of
California Santa Barbara (UCSB). This tool identifies appropriate zones for utility-scale solar and wind technologies in
developing countries.
The Solar PV and onshore wind zoning analysis was conducted using a combination of ArcGIS and ArcPy [112].
The zoning process is split up into the following stages.
Site Suitability Analysis.
In the first stage, we identify suitable sites for renewable energy deployment. Some basic
assumptions are made, such as a land use efficiency of 30%, as well as an average capacity factor (20% for both solar
and wind). In order to constrain where renewable projects can be built, many geographic features are excluded from
potential renewable sites – such as protected areas, urban zones, water bodies, steep slopes, and infrastructure buffers.
Each of these features is added as layers that include processing criteria that determine how each exclusion should be
applied spatially.
We process resource rasters (solar irradiation for solar PV and wind speed for wind turbines), with a threshold placed
to only keep locations where the resource available meets this threshold. The resource rasters are processed against
the geographic feature constraints, creating a map where suitable areas meet both the minimum resource threshold and
avoid all exclusion criteria. Areas smaller than a minimum threshold (2 km2 in this case) are filtered out.
Project Area.
This stage takes the suitable sites from the site suitability analysis and creates discrete project areas.
Whereas the sites in the previous stage are provided as continuous coverage across suitable terrain, we require renewable
development to occur in specific project boundaries. To accomplish this, we overlay a “fishnet grid” system on the
suitable areas.
This “fishnet grid” serves as a spatial framework for dividing large continuous suitable areas into
manageable project-sized parcels. In this process, areas above certain size thresholds are intersected with the “fishnet
grid” to create smaller parcels, while areas below minimum thresholds are aggregated with neighboring suitable areas
to create viable project sizes.
This spatial process ensures that the resulting project areas are neither too large for single development projects
nor too small to be economically viable. The configurable parameters in this stage are: grid cell size (5 km), minimum
contiguous area thresholds (2 km2), and maximum area limits (25 km2).
Zones Clustering.
The final stage groups individual project areas into larger development zones. We configured
the maximum area of the largest project to 100 km2 (for solar and for wind). This clustering process sums generation
capacity and energy production values while averaging cost and performance metrics, creating zones that represent
logical units for transmission planning and large-scale development. To avoid clustering that causes zones to be in
multiple states, we do not allow zones to exist across state boundaries.
We obtained 566 solar PV candidate zones and 311 onshore wind candidate zones. However, these projects include
zones located in the Andaman and Nicobar Islands, where the power grid is not connected to mainland India and thus
not included in our proposed GridPath-India model. After removing the candidate zones in the Andaman and Nicobar
Islands, we obtain the 556 solar PV and 300 onshore wind candidate zones in our GridPath-India model.
Offshore Wind Zones (REZoning).
The offshore wind candidate sites were selected using the Renewable Energy
Zoning tool (REZoning) [60]. REZoning is an online-based tool developed by the World Bank Group, Energy Sector
Management Assistance Program (ESMAP), and UCSB. The website provides a platform to identify suitable zones
for VRE projects and allows importing area shapefiles. This tool enables multi-criteria filtering based on topographic
limitations, land-use constraints, and system performance.
We define a 25 km resolution spatial grid. The distance of the candidate sites to a port is 0.5 ≥dport ≤50 km, to
an anchorage is danchorage ≤100 km, and to a UNESCO World Heritage Site is dUNESCO ≤500 km. The bathymetry
(i.e., distance to the ocean floor) must be ddepth ≥−1 km. In addition, we discarded protected areas, inertial areas,
coral reefs, and wetlands. The candidate sites resulting from this analysis are 46.

## Page 76


3
Capacity Factors
Solar.
The solar radiation measurements used to estimate the capacity factors for each solar Photovoltaic (PV)
candidate site are from the National Solar Radiation Database (NSRDB) [61], maintained by the National Renewable
Energy Laboratory (NREL). The PSM V3 model covers Europe, Africa, and Asia from 2017 to 2019. Both models are
publicly available. The PSM V3 has a spatial resolution of 4 × 4 km and is available in 15, 30, or 60 min temporal
resolution. We use the hourly temporal resolution (60 min). A METEOSAT IODC satellite acquired the data for the
model.
The capacity factors are simulated using the System Advisor Model (SAM ) developed by NREL as well [113]. The
capacity factors for solar PV in existing sites are assumed to be fixed-tilt, but new sites can be single-axis tracking or
fixed-tilt. The capacity expansion model uses data from Fiscal Year 2019 (FY19), Apr 2018 to Feb 2019. The resolution
available for solar radiation measurements is 4 km from 2017-2019. If a site is partially cloudy, 4 km is still a low
resolution to incorporate that information. In addition, the outage rates may be influential, and we did not make any
bias correction for solar PV sites at this spatial and temporal resolution. However, we verify that the average generation
simulated by SAM matches the generation from existing sites at the state level in FY19 (monthly/yearly).
We used PVWatts [64], available in the SAM Simulation Core (SSC) package for Python, PySAM [114]. The modules
on the fixed-tilt mount have an inclination angle equal to the candidate site’s latitude and have orientation to the source.
We have a solar PV roof mount profile (i.e., site) per state to simulate residential PV. Their tilt is the latitude of the
largest city in the state, and their orientation is south. The modules are assumed to be standard monocrystalline silicon
(19% efficiency). The modules mounted on a single-axis solar tracking system rotate horizontally. We assume that if
the user invests in a solar tracking system, they will also include the additional investment in premium monocrystalline
silicon modules (21% efficiency). The losses are higher for utility-scale projects (14%) than for residential projects (19%)
due to higher soling losses [65]. The inverter efficiency is the same (96%) for all PV systems.
Wind.
The wind velocity measurements are from FY19 since we have high spatial resolution solar PV capacity factors
from 2017 to 2019 in the PSM V3 model. However, we only have hourly wind velocity measurements with a low spatial
resolution (31 × 31 km) available in India from ERA5 [62]. The ERA5 weather reanalysis model has wind velocity
measurements available from 1940, but we need weather-synchronized capacity factors to calculate the capacity credits.
We need capacity factors from 2000 to 2014 to match the high spatial resolution solar radiation measurements available
in the SUNY model.
Wind velocity measurements in ERA5 have a bias [115]. We corrected the bias with the Global Wind Atlas (GWA)
to account for deviations in the capacity factors due to low resolution [63]. The GWA has a low temporal resolution,
average wind velocity at 100 m from 2008 to 2017, and a very high spatial resolution of 250 m. For that, we calculate
the bias in the average wind velocity at 100 m in the coordinates of each candidate site between the ERA5 and the
GWA. GWA uses data from 2008 to 2017, so we calculate the bias with wind speed data from ERA5 recorded in the
same years.
We used PySAM to simulate the capacity factors in the wind after bias-correcting the wind velocity measurements
[114]. The existing and new turbines are (site-specific optimal) commercial 2 MW Vestas V90, V100, or V110 (rated
for low, mid, and high wind velocity, respectively). The existing wind site capacity factors are calculated assuming high
outage rates (10%) and an 80 m height hub. The capacity factors for new wind sites have low outage rates (5%), and
120 m hub height. The capacity factors simulated for offshore wind sites have a low outage rate (5%) and 135 m high
hub height. The turbine is a commercial 7 MW Vestas V164 model with a 164 m diameter rotor, rated for high speeds.
The system losses are (14%) the same for all wind sites onshore and offshore.
We have seen actual generation data from sites in India, and the outage rates are high (sometimes weeks long). To
account for that, we increased the outage rate to 10% in existing wind sites. In addition, we want to ensure that the
aggregated generation from existing wind sites matches the actual generation and correct for the potential bias. This
second bias correction is made with FY19, which is the fiscal year used in the capacity expansion model. We calculate
the average capacity factor at the state level from the simulated and actual capacity factor time series of existing wind
sites in FY19. Then, we calculate the bias and correct the simulated capacity factor of each existing site to match the
state-level average capacity factors. We apply this second bias correction only to existing sites since the average wind
capacity factor at the national level is consistently increasing by approximately 0.5% every year from FY18 to FY23.
Hydroelectric.
The capacity factors for hydro storage and Run-Of-River (ROR) are from the annual generation
reports FY19 to synchronize them with the weather. The reports provide generation monthly for 94 hydro ROR and
84 hydro storage power plants with their respective nameplate capacity. We aggregate the monthly energy generation
across the load zones for both hydroelectric technologies and divide it by the hydroelectric technology total nameplate
capacity in the load zone to calculate monthly capacity factors.
We define the balancing horizon for hydroelectric storage projects as a month, so GridPath uses these monthly
76


## Page 77


capacity factors to calculate their respective monthly budgets [17]. However, hydro ROR generation is VRE and thus is
not dispatchable. GridPath requires hourly capacity factors to simulate their electricity dispatch. We assign the average
monthly capacity factor to all hours in the month for simplification.
Biomass.
Biomass is mainly from agricultural residues in India. The agricultural residues available amount depends
on the harvest. The biomass generation depends on the crops grown in the area near the power station. In particular,
bagasse is a biomass byproduct of sugar production extracted from sugarcane juice. The Central Energy Agency (CEA)
reports biomass and bagasse generation separately. We model bagasse capacity within the biomass capacity. The reports
contain monthly biomass and bagasse generation at the state level. The CEA also updates the existing capacity of the
biomass and bagasse in the reports.
We calculate the capacity factors from the reports published for FY20. As we do not propose new biomass projects,
we consider biomass generation at the state level to have the necessary granularity. However, the capacity factors are
from monthly generation since we do not have hourly generation data, which is the required temporal resolution. We
assign the average monthly capacity factor to each hour in the days of the month.

4
India-Specific Technology Cost Projections
New Generator Cost Projections.
The Photovoltaic (PV) with single-axis tracking system cost projections are
the base utility-scale estimates in the Annual Technology Baseline (ATB) from 2023 [82]. We linearly adjust them to
match the auction prices from the International Renewable Energy Agency (IRENA) in 2020 [83]. The 2022 Indian
Technology Catalogue (ITC) estimates are lower than the auction prices in 2020 [81], we assume them to be biased
toward lower costs, so we use the mid-cost as the lower bound (2035 and 2050) and the high cost as the upper bound
(2050), which matches the adjusted 2023 ATB estimates (2050). The upper bound estimate for 2035 is the average
between the 2022 ITC (2035) and the adjusted 2023 ATB (2035). The mid estimate is the average between the upper
and lower bounds (Figure 28A). We apply a standard 1-dimensional signal processing technique [116], Piecewise Cubic
Hermit Interpolation Polynomial (PCHUP), to interpolate the trends (Table 29).

Figure 28: New generator and energy storage cost projections. The gray lines represent the technology cost projections
from the literature. The solid lines are the proposed technology cost projections in our study. The different colors represent a
technology. The spread between the low and high-cost projections is shaded in the technology color. New renewable generator
cost projections for solar PV on a single-axis tracking system (A), fixed-tilt structure (B), and mounted on a roof (C), and for
wind offshore (D) and onshore (E). New short-term storage cost projections for PSH power capacity (G) and energy capacity
(J), and battery power capacity (H) and energy capacity (K). New long-term storage cost projections (hydrogen) are for PEM
electrolyzer (F), H2 fuel cell (I), and salt cavern storage (L).
The 2023 ATB and the 2022 ITC do not provide cost estimates for solar PV with fixed tilt for utility-scale projects
78


## Page 79


[81, 82]. A recent investigation provides an estimation of the ratio between the cost of both PV technologies in utility-
scale plants in the US in 2022 [84]. We assume this relation is the same in India, and linearly adjust our solar PV
single-axis cost projections to approximate the cost of solar PV fixed-tilt technology (Figure 28B and Table 29).
The solar PV roof-mount cost projections are based on the individual components in the 2022 ITC [81].
The
hypotensions in low cost scenario in the 2022 ITC is insistent with the literature and consequentially rejected. The mid
scenario is assumed the lower cost scenario. We independently adjusted the cost projections of each component using
the methodology in the 2023 ATB [85]. We linearly adjusted the cost projection of the modules in India applying a scale
factor between utility-scale and residential modules in the US. Similarly, the cost projection of the inverter by linearly
adjusting the inverter and electrical hardware costs. The initial costs in 2020 are from the upper scenario. The lower
and mid scenario are set to start with this same initial cost (Figure 28C and Table 29). The mid scenario is the average
between the new high and low cost scenarios. We apply PCHUP to interpolate the trend from 2020 to 2030. The mid
estimate is the average between the upper and lower bounds.

Onshore wind cost projections combine information on declining trends from the 2023 ATB with auction prices for
onshore wind in India from the IRENA database [82, 83]. The 2023 ATB projections are linearly adjusted to match the
auction prices in 2020. The adjusted 2023 ATB projections are higher in 2020 and lower in 2030 than the 2022 ITC cost
estimates [81]. Due to this contradiction, we do not trust the adjusted trend from the 2023 ATB. Similarly, 2022 ITC
projections for 2050 seem conservative compared to international declining trends. Therefore, we only use the initial
cost in the trend (2020) and the estimations from the original 2023 ATB as intermediate and terminal points in the
series (2030 and 2050, respectively); see Figure 28D and Table 30. We also use PCHUP to interpolate the trends.
Offshore wind cost projections combine information on declining trends from the 2023 ATB and the 2022 ITC [81,
82]. The IRENA database does not have offshore wind auction prices in India. Therefore, the 2023 ATB is linearly
adjusted using local auction prices for onshore wind. ITC offshore wind cost projections align with the upper bound of
adjusted 2023 ATB projections in 2020. We use them to define the initial cost. We then use the 2022 ITC projection
79

projections are the upper bound (2020, 2035, and 2050); see Figure 28E and Table 30. The mid estimate is the average
between the upper and lower estimates. Similarly, we apply PCHUP to interpolate the trends.

The new coal (supercritical) and gas Combustion Turbine (CT) or Combined Cycle Gas Turbine (CCGT) projects
are considered mature technologies. The cost projections are constant from 2020 to 2050. The coal costs are local
economic proposals for new projects, and information is only available upon request. The mid and high costs in coal
projects represent uncertainty in construction and equipment procurement. The CT from the 2022 ITC [81]. The 2022
ITC costs for CCGT are below the market price and lower than CT estimates and thus discarded. However, an upper
bound for the CT estimations is available in the 2022 ITC; we use this estimation as the mid-scenario for CCGT. The
cost projections for a new nuclear project is from the 2022 ITC [81]. Nuclear is a mature technology, we assume the
same costs from 2020 to 2050 (Table 31).

New Energy Storage Cost Projections.
The storage technologies investigated in this study are Pumped Storage
Hydropower (PSH), Lithium-Ion batteries, and hydrogen. PSH and batteries are considered short-term storage, and
hydrogen is long-term storage. The charge and discharge of short-term storage are optimized with a daily horizon, and
long-term storage has a yearly optimization horizon.
The cost of PSH in a project is uncertain and highly depends on local economic factors, geographic conditions, and
weather patterns [81, 117]. We have information about the costs in the 2023 ATB [82] and local estimations for PSH
on-river and off-river in 2022 ITC and 2024 ITC-PS [81, 86]. 2023 ATB and the 2024 ITC Power Storage (PS) consider
PSH a mature technology, and the costs will not decrease from 2020 to 2050. These sources provide the cost in terms
of the power component in a PSH plant, and similarly to batteries, GridPath-India requires the cost of the power and
energy components in a PSH project.
Another study provides the cost for the different components in a PSH project, but in the US [87]. Additionally, we
have information about the auctioned PSH projects in India in 2023 [88], and we have costs for distinctly low (“Kurukutti
PSH MP30”), mid (“Gandhi Sagar PSH”), and high (“Ghatghar PSH”) cost projects. We linearly adjust the power and
energy capacity costs (from the US) to derive low, mid, and high cost estimates with the auction information from India
(Figure 28G and J, and Table 32). We consider PSH a mature technology similar to the 2023 ATB and 2024 ITC-PS.
Therefore, we assume the cost projections from 2020 to 2030 are constant.

The battery cost combines information from the 2024 ITC-PS and the 2022 ATB [86, 89]. We do not use the 2023
ATB for batteries since India did not observe the projected cost increase in 2024 [82]. The 2022 ATB cost projections
are in terms of the power capacity (kW) of a 4 h duration storage, and GridPath-India optimizes power (kW) and
energy (kWh) capacity. We calculate the cost projections for multiple storage times (2 h, 4 h, 6 h, and 8 h) and 1 MW
and estimate the relation between energy (battery) and power capacity (electronic) in the 2022 ATB projections; see
Figure 28H and K. Then, we implement a method available in the literature to adjust the battery cost projections
in the ATB to cost projections in India, based on international auctions [90]; see Figure 28H and K. However, recent
battery auctions in the IRENA database show that the battery cost in 2020 [83] was lower than the lower bound in 2050
estimated following this method. We linearly adjust the projections to incorporate this information (Figure 28H and K).
The power capacity component of these projections is still much lower than the 2024 ITC-PS [86], and the energy
capacity component is much higher (Figure 28H and K). This shows a great disparity between the projections from
different sources and the actual auction costs. Due to this, we are inclined not to trust these projections and relay
auction costs, and ongoing tenders (FDRE, RTC, and GUVNL BESS) show that these cost estimates will also be high
in 2030 [91]. We linearly adjust the upper and lower bounds from 2030 to 2050 and interpolate the trends using PCHUP
from 2020. The mid estimate is the average between the upper and lower bounds (Table 33).
We assume hydrogen will be generated from water via electrolysis with a Proton Exchange Membrane (PEM)
electrolyzer [55]. The hydrogen is liquified and stored in tanks or nonporous caverns, and the hydrogen is oxidized in
fuel cells to generate electricity and water again. As of today, hydrogen open-cycle combustion turbines are cheaper than
fuel cells [92]. However, hydrogen storage will not be a feasible alternative until 2030 or 2040, when the cost projections
for fuel cells are lower than for combustion turbines.
The PEM electrolyzer base costs are from the 2024 ITC Green Fuels (GF) [93], but do not provide uncertainty. We
also have prospect studies projections with upper and lower bounds [118, 119]. The initial point is NREL’s projection
(2020), similar to the 2024 ITC-GF (2020). The prospect provides extremely low [119] and high estimates in 2020 [118].
We discard these estimates and focus on the uncertainty bounded by the region between the mid and low estimates
in [118] and the upper and mid estimates in [119] from 2030 to 2050. We define our lower bound (2035) as the lower
bound in [119] and our upper bound as their mid estimates in 2035 and 2050. Our lower bound is linearly adjusted to
match local auction prices for electrolyzers expected to come online before 2030 without considering incentives [120];
see Figure 28F and Table 34.
We used international projections of hydrogen storage in salt caverns from the US [87] and Denmark per lack of
local information [94]; see Figure 28L. Previous studies have independently trusted these projections [95]. However, we
propose to combine both studies. These projections have a similar initial cost in 2020, but estimations for 2050 are
considerably different. The lower estimates from the DEA are very similar to their mid-cost, so we use the mid-cost as
our lower estimate for simplification [94]. The low cost from the US matches the mid-cost from the DEA. However, US

high-cost estimates are 5 times larger than the mid-cost and thus inconsistent with the rest of the literature [87]. We
define US estimates as high projections and DEA as low projections, and use their average as our mid projections. We
interpolate upper, mid, and lower trends using PCHUP to create monotonically decreasing trends (Table 34).
The fuel cell projections follow the upper bound proposed in previous studies from 2020 to 2050 [121]. The mid-cost
projections (2020-2030) are from targets set by the Department of Energy (DOE) and use actual fuel cell costs [96].
Notice that the initial projections based on the actual cost in 2020 considerably differ from previous studies [119]. The
lower bound is the same lower bound from a recent study from 2030 to 2050 [122]. We do not use this lower bound from
2020 to 2030, as it considerably differs from the DOE study with actual costs. The mid projections from 2030 to 2050
are the average between the lower and upper bounds (Figure 28I and Table 34). We interpolate the points by applying
PCHUP to define the trends.
New Project Operational Characteristics.
The Operation and Maintenance (O&M) cost projections are from
international estimates available on the 2023 ATB, the 2022 ITC, the 2024 ITC-PS, and the 2024 ITC-GF [81, 82, 86,
93]. We only consider mid-cost projections and the costs are assumed to decrease slightly from 2020 to 2050 for emerging
technologies (hydrogen, storage, solar PV, and wind), except for offshore since the 2023 ATB indicates a considerable
decrease [82], and the battery is steady (due to an increase in labor decrease in technology cost). The O&M cost from
mature technologies is expected to increase (coal and nuclear) or remain constant (CT, CCGT, and PSH). In particular,
we consider O&M capacity cost (USD/kW) for all technologies and O&M energy cost (USD/kWh) for battery storage
(Table 29-34). We assume PSH for the energy component (USD/kWh) included in the power component. We assume
the O&M cost for a hydrogen tank and cavern storage is equivalent they are in the PEM electrolyzer and fuel cell O&M
costs.

generators are publicly available in state-level tariff orders or published by the Ministry of Power through the “Merit

Order of Despatch for the Rejuvenation of Income and Transparency (MERIT) program”. The fuel and variable O&M
costs (USD/MWh) for a new thermal project are the average of the generators with the same fuel type in the state of
the project. However, we consider that a new gas project will not consume local gas. The cost of gas is uncertain, but we

83


## Page 84


rate of 9% and aggregated the fixed O&M cost projections (kW/yr) for each technology. The minimum capacity factor
and minimum up/down time are generic values for thermal generators (coal, gas, nuclear). CT technology is more
flexible than CCGT. Storage efficiencies and project lifetime are current estimates and do not consider improvements
over time. We assume different start-up cost (USD/MWh) from the 2022 ITC for coal (subcritical small and large, and
supercritical) and gas (CT and CGT) projects but the same for existing and new projects [81]; see Table 35 and 36.
Specified Projects.
The annualized capital investment and the fuel plus variable costs O&M (USD/MWh) for each
specified thermal project (coal, gas, and oil) are publicly available through the MERIT program. This research considers
generations scheduled to come online between 2020 and 2025 (nuclear, coal, and hydroelectric).
The hydroelectric
projects in the pipeline are ROR. For these projects, we consider 2022 ITC costs [81]; see Table 36. Similarly, specified
biomass/bagasse is from 2022 ITC costs [81]. This investigation does not differentiate between bagasse and biomass
projects. We did not find concise information for Waste Heat Recovery (WHR) projects in India [123]. Thus, we use
the information from a recently published viability study for WHR projects in Pakistan. We do not propose any new
project on top of the capacity in the pipeline for ROR technology.

## Page 85
