Demand Projection Scenarios
The demand projections are based on the model Perspectives on Indian Energy based on Rumi version 2.0 (PIER v2.0),
which estimates the electricity demand from cost-optimal supply options at the wide energy system level [19]. PIER
v2.0 demand projections are alternative to the demand projections published on the Electric Power Survey (EPS) [124].
The main difference is in the assumption of the solarization of the agricultural sector in PIER v2.0 projections and
climate change impacts on the residential sector. PIER v2.0 demand projections include state-level captive generation
demand projections.
The temperature projections used in PIER v2.0 for the bottom-up demand projections for the residential sector are
based on the SSP2-4.5 scenario [125, 126], from the IPSL-CM6A-LR climate model [127], available on the CMIP6 dataset
[128]. The SSP2-4.5 scenario combines the Shared Socioeconomic Pathway 2 (SSP2) development scenario (“middle of
the road”), with the assumption that Earth’s surface will receive an additional increase of 4.5W/m2 radiation forcing by
2100. This scenario assumes development and income growth persist in being unequal, leading to a moderate increase
in GHG emissions by 2050 and eventually achieving net-zero by 2100.
The temperature projections from the SSP2-4.5 scenario are used to determine population-weighted cooling degree
hours and cooling hours for households modeled at the state urban/rural granularity. The cooling degree hours and
cooling hours are used to determine electricity demand due to air conditioners and fans/coolers, respectively, based on
the appliance characteristics. Climate impacts are not considered in any other sectors.
PIER v2.0 demand projections are at the consumer (i.e., ex-bus), and do not consider transmission and distribution
(T&D) losses. Projections of transmission losses are available at the state level. We project them to FY50 and linearly
adjust the projections to account for different transmission losses for each FY and state. The losses are projected to
FY50 based on past trends of loss reduction, assuming a maximum reduction rate of 3% per year to reach a minimum
T&D loss of 7% at the state level. The historical T&D loss data is available in the “All India Electricity Statistics -
General Review” reports [99].
GridPath-India includes transmission corridors and losses due to energy exchanges. It does not account for all losses
at the transmission and distribution level. The transmission losses modeled by GridPath-India are approximately 2.5%
at the national level. We subtract this number from the transmission losses projections (before linearly adjusting the
profiles) to properly characterize the additional generation necessary to balance the transmission losses.
We have three scenarios in PIER v2.0. The different narratives are explained below.
• “Reference” (mid growth): This is a combination of the sector-specific reference scenarios, and represents our best
guess of what is likely to happen in the future based on past and likely future trends.
• “Vikasit Bharat” (high growth): This scenario can represent the equivalent of a sustainable development scenario.
It reflects the aspirations of a developed India by 2047 (FY48) announced by the Government of India. We assume
that this also means a more equitable development, with greater focus on sustainability.
• “Vichalit Bharat” (low growth): This scenario is a counterpoint to “Vikasit Bharat” and represents a scenario in
which development is a bit more haphazard. Economic growth is lower than the “Reference”, it is less equitable,
and it is also less environmentally sustainable due to lower investments in efficiency and new technologies.
Further details about the bottom-up assumptions in each economic sector for each scenario (“Vikasit Bharat”, “Refer-
ence”, and “Vichalit Bharat”) in PIER v2.0 are in Table 37.

Capacity Credits
The top net-load hours (TNLH) method is used to approximate the capacity credits (CC) for VRE assets (i.e., projects).
For this method, it is necessary to implement a 2-year resource adequacy study based on the cumulative distribution of
the net-load to approximate capacity credits of a VRE asset. We propose an 18-year resource adequacy to improve the
accuracy.
To implement this approach, we first sort the net load timepoints (τ = {1, . . . , 8760}) in each resource adequacy
year (y = {1, . . . , 18}) in descending order. Then, we select the first T = 250 samples, and obtain the capacity Bγ,y,π
and generation Oγ,y,π of each VRE generator γ ∈Γω in a given investment period π. Then, we calculate the NLCC
capacity creditγ,y,π of project γ, and average for each year y,

capacity creditγ for generator γ is equivalent to the average capacity factors (capacity factorγ,τ ′,y,π) on timepoints τ ′
in year y.
Weather-Synchronized Capacity factors Time Series.
We utilize weather data from FY19 to weather-synchronize
the capacity factors with the demand time series. However, estimating the capacity credits requires weather data from
multiple years [129].
As the solar radiation data from the PSM V3 model is only available from 2017 to 2019, we combined these data
with data from the SUNY International model available from 2010 to 2014 and maintained by NREL [61]. SUNY has a
spatial resolution of 10 × 10 km and hourly temporal resolution (60 min). The spatial resolution is lower than PSM V3
but higher than ERA5 [62], so we assume we do not need bias correction. However, the onshore and offshore time series
are from ERA5 (2010 to 2014 and 2017 to 2019), and we applied the same bias correction explained in Section 3.
In addition to wind speed, we use other weather features from ERA5 (precipitation, temperature, humidity, and
atmospheric pressure) combined with elevation data from GMTED2010 maintained by U.S. Geological Survey [130] to
predict hydro ROR capacity factor time series and hydro storage month availability for 2010 to 2014 and 2017 to 2019.
The proposed model combines topological details and weather information to apply a distance-based weight and learn
local non-linear water runoff and evaporation rate patterns.
Weather-Synchronized Demand Time Series.
The demand profiles also require weather synchronization to cal-
culate the capacity credits effectively. We utilized the method proposed in Section 6 to generate profiles for 2030, 2040,
and 2050 from PIER projections, and we weather synchronized with the demand profiles from FY18 and FY19 provided
by ICED [100]. The demand profile synchronization predicts state-level demand profiles with the weather features from
ERA5 utilized to predict the hydro ROR and storage capacity factors. In this case, we proposed a model that combines
the weather features with the “Gridded Population of the World” dataset (GPWv4) [131] to weight the observations in
the learning function by space coordinate with a higher population.
90


## Page 91


8
Operational Reserves Projections
The reserve requirements are determined based on the procedure available in “Detailed Procedure on Interim Method-
ology for Estimation of Reserves Under CERC (Ancillary Services) Regulations, 2022” [110].
In this manual, the
description of the secondary and tertiary reserves is equivalent to the spinning reserves (Freq) and frequency response
(Spin) functionalities available in GridPath. The secondary and tertiary reserve requirements are specified quarterly by
CEA. The requirements are defined at the state-level (i.e., load zone z) and regional-level (r = {1, · · · , 5}).
We calculate the reserve requirements based on secondary and tertiary reserves estimation implemented in the
“Quarter-Ahead SRAS and TRAS Reserve Requirement for Quarter 2 (July - September) of the Year 2023-24 (Scaled
for Reference Contingency of 4500 MW)” [132]. In this report, the secondary reserves are determined based on the
positive and negative regulations in the last quarter, the peak demand, and the state generation at the peak demand.
The tertiary reverses are determined based on the secondary reserves and the largest unit capacity in that state. This
information is not available for future fiscal years, so we assume unit capacity remains constant—thus no larger units will
be commissioned in future investment periods— and that the magnitude of the regulations (i.e., net demand forecasting
error) will grow as a function of the peak demand for simplification. Additionally, we project the reserve requirements
for each investment period, instead of quarterly.
Therefore, we propose to model the state-level secondary reserves in GridPath-India as frequency regulation (Freq).
We calculate Freqτ,π,z with the ratio between the state (i.e., load zone) secondary reserves requirement in the FY24 Jul.
to Sep. report and the peak load ρz = Freqπ=F Y 24,z/ max [Dτ,π=F Y 24,z] for each zone (z) in the investment periods (π)
from FY20 to FY50. In this way, we project the secondary reserves for each time point (τ) as
Freqτ,π,z = ρz · ˜Dτ,π,z,

in their daily outage reports. We have historical outage reports from multiple periods (π⋆= {2018, . . . , 2023}). The
availability factors (availability factorγ,τ,π⋆) for a generator γ with capacity (Bγ) can be derived from the outage rates,
